#5156. TD Cowen: Democratic Congress Would Sink CLARITY Act for Two Years

TD Cowen’s Jaret Seiberg says a Democratic sweep of Congress would kill the CLARITY Act for two years, hand Elizabeth Warren the Banking Committee and let Democrats block Trump’s picks for the SEC and CFTC.

A Democratic sweep of Congress would end the CLARITY Act’s chances for the next two years and sour the political climate for crypto, TD Cowen’s Washington analyst Jaret Seiberg wrote to Bitcoin Magazine and clients in an election-day note.

Seiberg wrote that TD Cowen agrees with polls and prediction markets that Democrats are likely to take both chambers, with the caveat that polls taken a month out, which were more mixed, tend to beat those taken on the eve of a vote.

His conclusion for crypto is blunt: “Democrats will likely investigate Trump including his crypto entanglements. We see this as souring the political environment for crypto, which is why we do not expect the Clarity Act to pass over the next two years.”

The market structure bill passed the House 294-134 in July 2025 and failed a Senate cloture vote 49-50 on Sept. 15. If it does not pass before the new Congress convenes on Jan. 3, it dies and must be reintroduced.

Seiberg added that crypto’s influence may shrink further in Congress if Sherrod Brown wins his Ohio Senate race, a contest the industry spent to lose.

The note treats Senate control as the bigger prize. A Democratic majority could block Trump’s nominees and use that power to force him to fill Democratic seats on the FDIC, SEC and CFTC, the three agencies at the center of crypto policy.

It could pressure the White House to drop its effort to remove Lisa Cook from the Federal Reserve board. Trump could answer with acting chiefs, Seiberg wrote, but that risks handing authority to career staff, an outcome “Team Trump” does not want.

Senate control would elevate Sen. Elizabeth Warren to chair the Banking Committee. Seiberg sees room for populist bills such as interest rate caps, limits on bank buybacks and a government credit score, and he thinks Warren and Trump could find common ground on some of them.

Rep. Maxine Waters would take House Financial Services, with a focus on housing and tighter scrutiny of the FHFA.

“That means a focus on housing though we are dubious that she will find GOP support for down payment help and public housing renovations. FHFA scrutiny will increase,” the note read.

For banks, the note says the election changes little: regulators will keep easing capital, liquidity and tiering rules whichever party wins. Divided government does raise the odds of a shutdown or a debt-ceiling standoff, since Trump is expected to reject spending conditions Democrats attach.

Bitcoin traded near $77,500 Tuesday afternoon eastern time, up from a dip to $76,300 in the morning and about $2,000 below where it opened the month. The price has absorbed a record $6.34 billion Binance hack, two days of spot ETF outflows and a Fed that is leaning toward a December hike.

Prediction markets on Kalshi give Democrats a 94% chance at the House and 74% at the Senate, so much of Seiberg’s scenario is in the price.

What is not priced is the lame-duck session. Sen. Angela Alsobrooks, a CLARITY negotiator, has said the bill could pass before January even if a Democratic Congress would not make it a priority after. That said, if it fails during that window, she doubts a Democratic-controlled Congress will make it a priority in the future.

According to various reports, crypto groups have spent more than $200 million on the cycle to avoid the outcome Seiberg now expects. The CFTC, for its part, moved without Congress on Tuesday, clearing equity perpetual futures for U.S. exchanges. If the Senate and Congress flips, that agency-by-agency approach, and who sits on those agencies, becomes the whole game.

#5154. CZ calls for crypto security alliance as AI-driven attacks accelerate after Binance hack

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Changpeng Zhao says companies need AI-based defenses and an industry alliance to share lessons and freeze stolen funds faster, in his first interview since the Oct. 29 hack.

Binance cofounder and former CEO Changpeng "CZ" Zhao said AI is accelerating cyberattacks and argued companies should invest in AI-based defenses, security personnel, and partnerships with AI security providers.

In his first public interview since the Oct. 29 hack of Binance, Zhao told the Talking Tokens podcast that the threat extends beyond individual companies to the broader crypto industry and global financial system, given crypto's connections to stablecoins, tokenized stocks, real-world assets, and traditional finance.

"The speed of hacking is going to pick up because of AI capabilities [being] so strong now. At the same time, we've got to use more AI to defend. The only way to fight a monster is to kind of become that monster."

The comments come amid a string of security incidents across the crypto industry, including a separate AI-assisted breach at blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis and a $46 million security incident at Brazilian exchange Mercado Bitcoin.

Zhao said he learned of the incident around midnight in the UAE and focused on communicating with the community while Binance's team worked to contain the attack and protect users. He attributed relatively calm market conditions and subsequent net inflows to the company's response, financial capacity and user trust. He described security incidents as preventable with sufficient effort and attention, while warning that organizations can become complacent.

Earlier Tuesday, Binance said hackers used a fake job interview to compromise an employee's computer before leveraging an internal AI tool in the attack. The exchange also said it detected and prevented 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threats in 2026.

Zhao called for an industry alliance to share lessons and best practices, conduct cross-audits and help freeze stolen funds more quickly. He views security as an ongoing process rather than a milestone that must be reached before adoption.

"As an industry, I encourage [...] forming some kind of industry alliance on security to help each other learn from each other's mistakes, share the best practices, cross audits, help each other freeze funds very quickly, etc."

Zhao said he had not spoken with President Donald Trump or other U.S. officials about the incident, but that Binance and the broader crypto industry would welcome government assistance. Trump has said the U.S. was "looking into it."

"What's bad for crypto on this scale has real economic impact elsewhere," Zhao said. "So you cannot just say, 'Look, this is one specific problem for one specific sector or for one specific company.' It impacts the company, impacts the industry, impacts global finance. It's all interconnected, and I think, given the size of impact, it's definitely at the national security level as well."

Zhao expressed optimism that AI could help identify software vulnerabilities and strengthen defenses, while blockchain could support faster transactions facilitated by AI.

#5152. Ethereum Privacy Network Aztec Rolls Out Major Security and Speed Upgrade

The Aztec Foundation said Tuesday that the upgrade, called Aztec Tierra and designated Alpha V6, delivers stronger security, formal verification of its proving system and faster deposits from Ethereum, alongside a refreshed brand.

Aztec is a decentralized layer-2 with more than 3,000 validators and fully programmable private smart contracts, and it pitches itself as having no privileged viewing keys or centralized sequencer, meaning no single operator can peer into or control user activity.

The upgrade resolves known vulnerabilities from earlier versions, including a critical issue discovered in V5, and adds extensive hardening. Notably, that includes a new AI-powered security testing harness and formal verification of core cryptographic components, a mathematical process for proving code behaves as intended.

The move reflects a broader scramble across crypto to harden code as AI tools make vulnerabilities easier to find.

On performance, Aztec said private transactions now generate client-side proofs in about one second on a standard device, twice as fast as before, while a new architecture cuts the time for Ethereum-to-Aztec messages from an average of two minutes to as little as 12 seconds, speeding up deposits and cross-chain activity.

The upgrade arrives with a crop of private applications spanning payments, trading, AI and social.

Leading them is zk.money, a private payments and savings app built by Aztec Labs that the team revived this year. With Tierra’s faster messaging, users can deposit directly from any Ethereum wallet without complex bridging.

Others in the lineup include Galactica for private AI inference, Nemi for private perpetuals trading, a private browser wallet called Nulo, and Rolling Chat, a private message board based on Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin’s own Aztec experiments.

#5151. SEC division views Canton Coin as digital commodity in no-action letter

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

a16z partner Miles Jennings welcomed the SEC staff's position, arguing it could signal a broader regulatory path for blockchain network tokens.

The U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission's Division of Corporation Finance said it views Canton Coin (CC) as a digital commodity and will not recommend enforcement action over its programmatic issuance under certain conditions.

In a no-action letter published Tuesday, the division said Canton Coin derives its value from the programmatic operation of the Canton Network and supply-and-demand dynamics, rather than expectations of profit from the managerial efforts of others. It also cited the network's lack of a central party.

"Based on the facts presented, the Division will not recommend enforcement action to the Commission if, in reliance on your opinion as counsel, Canton Coin is minted programmatically by Super Validators, Validators and application providers, in the manner and under the circumstances described in your letter, without registration under Section 5 of the Securities Act or Section 12(g) of the Exchange Act," Deputy Chief Counsel Jonathan Ingram wrote.

The letter reflects the Division of Corporation Finance's enforcement position based on the specific facts and representations submitted by Canton and does not express a legal conclusion on the issues presented, including whether Canton Coin is a security.

In May, 21Shares became the first U.S. ETF issuer to offer direct exposure to CC, the network's native utility token.

In June, Digital Asset raised $355 million in a funding round led by Andreessen Horowitz's a16z crypto fund to expand the Canton ecosystem and accelerate institutional adoption of its blockchain infrastructure.

a16z partner Miles Jennings welcomed the SEC staff's position, arguing it could signal a broader regulatory path for blockchain network tokens.

"Incredible. @CantonNetwork just received no action relief from the SEC for its network token," Jennings wrote Tuesday on X. "The @SECGov's token taxonomy from March and follow up FAQs have now created a clear pathway out of securities laws—Network tokens are digital commodities."

#5149. Tether Holds 150,000 Bitcoin CEO Says, 50,000 More Than Its Reserve Report Shows

Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino said in Lugano that the company holds about 150,000 bitcoin worth $11.5 billion.

Tether holds about 150,000 bitcoin, worth some $11.5 billion at current prices, CEO Paolo Ardoino said, a figure that exceeds the holdings implied by the company’s latest reserve attestation by roughly 50,000 coins.

“We have give or take 150,000 bitcoin ourselves. It’s not much, but it’s honest work,” Ardoino said during his keynote at the Plan ₿ Forum in Lugano, Switzerland, the city where Tether has run a bitcoin and stablecoin adoption program since 2022.

At that size the stack would rank Tether among the largest corporate holders of bitcoin, behind Strategy’s 848,517 BTC (as of Q3) and ahead of most public miners and treasury companies.

Ardoino did not say where the additional coins sit, when they were bought or whether they belong to the stablecoin issuer or to Tether Investments, the arm that holds the group’s venture and equity positions. The remark does not indicate a new 50,000 BTC purchase.

JUST IN: Tether now holds approximately 150,000 Bitcoin worth $11.5 billion, CEO Paolo Ardoino announces 👀 pic.twitter.com/s7HLmXwkQE

Tether International’s third-quarter reserve report, published Oct. 30 with an attestation from BDO, lists $8.37 billion in bitcoin valued at $83,621.23 per coin as of Sept. 30. That implies about 100,060 BTC, up around 1,130 from the roughly 98,930 held at the end of June. Most of the quarter’s $2.57 billion rise in the bitcoin line came from price, not purchases.

The gap between the report and Ardoino’s figure has a plausible home. Tether has said the investments it makes from profits are kept apart from the reserves backing USDT, so coins held by Tether Investments would not appear in the attestation.

Tether’s bitcoin exposure extends past its own wallets. It took full control of Twenty One Capital in May after buying out SoftBank, backs Adecoagro’s mining in Brazil, and runs mining and energy ventures in Latin America. Whether any of those positions sit inside the 150,000 figure is unclear.

The disclosure lands as Tether’s balance sheet grows more dependent on the two assets. The Q3 report shows $191.45 billion in reserve assets against $183.50 billion in liabilities, an excess of $7.95 billion, up from $4.11 billion in June when the cushion had halved.

Bitcoin at $8.37 billion and gold at about $19.1 billion together exceed that buffer several times over, so a sharp drop in either would eat into it. Treasury bills remain the bulk of reserves at about $132.4 billion.

Tether earned about $1.5 billion in the quarter and $4.5 billion in the first nine months. In August it completed its first independent audit with KPMG, ending years of reliance on attestations alone. Ardoino said at the forum that USDT has more than 730 million users and is adding about 30 million wallets a quarter.

Bitcoin traded near $77,600 Tuesday, which puts Tether’s stated holdings at $11.6 billion and its attested holdings at about $7.8 billion, below the Sept. 30 mark. The company has not said whether it plans to publish a breakdown of the coins held outside the reserve.

#5148. Binance’s net inflows turn positive at $2.3B as withdrawals expand

Binance led exchanges in 24-hour net inflows, reversing Monday’s heavy outflows as it restored access to funds after its $6.3 billion hack.

Binance recorded $2.3 billion in net inflows over the past 24 hours as it expanded withdrawals following last week’s $6.3 billion hack.

The exchange led centralized exchanges tracked by DefiLlama in 24-hour net inflows. Bitfinex recorded about $280 million and Bitget about $127 million over the same period.

“By 03:00 UTC, we are seeing hourly deposits exceed withdrawals, reflecting improving user activity and stabilizing flows,” Binance said Tuesday on X.

The inflows mark a reversal from Monday, when Binance recorded more than $7 billion in 24-hour net outflows. OKX led exchanges with about $445 million in net inflows at that time.

Binance suspended withdrawals on Friday after Thursday’s $6.3 billion hack while it investigated the breach and conducted security checks. It began restoring withdrawals at 06:00 UTC on Sunday and reopened withdrawals for some fiat currencies on Monday, as outflows started to slow down.

In a Sunday recovery update, Binance said it had replaced approximately 95% of the affected assets. The remaining 5% consisted of less liquid tokens that took longer to acquire through market and over-the-counter purchases.

The exchange has pledged to cover the losses using company resources and protection funds without passing them on to customers.

The US Federal Bureau of Investigation attributed the attack to North Korea on Saturday. North Korea denied responsibility Tuesday, with Kim Yo Jong, the sister of leader Kim Jong Un, dismissing the allegation as “absurd rhetoric.”

#5146. South Korea’s ruling party formally proposes deferral of crypto income tax

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

If approved, the proposal would mark the fourth postponement of the levy, which was first legislated in 2020.

South Korea's ruling party leader has officially proposed that the government further delay the crypto tax.

Democratic Party leader Kim Min-seok said at a party supreme council meeting at the National Assembly that the decision followed consultations through the Strategy and Finance Committee, government-party talks, and meetings with crypto asset businesses, local news outlet News1 reported on Wednesday.

Kim stated that party members agreed that the country should first enact the Digital Asset Basic Act, a comprehensive legal framework for crypto, and give companies and investors time to adapt to new rules.

The tax plan, currently scheduled to take effect on Jan. 1, 2027, would impose a 22% tax (including local tax) on annual gains from the transfer or lending of crypto assets above a basic deduction of 2.5 million won ($1,868.7). If approved, the proposal would mark the fourth postponement of the levy, which was first legislated in 2020.

The ruling party leader acknowledged past deferrals of the tax and said that this time the party would set a clear deferral period while thoroughly preparing prerequisites, including use of overseas transaction information, IT system verification and taxpayer guidance.

Last week, South Korea's presidential office was reportedly discussing postponement of the levy until 2029 to align with the U.S. timeline for joining the OECD's Crypto-Asset Reporting Framework.

#5145. UK Says AI-Powered Ransomware Attack on Critical Infrastructure 'Highly Likely' by 2028

UK Says AI-Powered Ransomware Attack on Critical Infrastructure 'Highly Likely' by 2028

Britain's critical national infrastructure should be braced for a ransomware attack involving AI-powered malware by 2028, the UK's National Cyber Security Centre warned on Tuesday.

Within the next two years, hostile actors' use of AI is "highly likely" to increase the scale and efficiency of their operations, including ransomware, social engineering and the exploitation of software vulnerabilities, according to the NCSC's annual State of the Cyber Nation report. The agency is part of GCHQ, the UK's signals intelligence service.

Today, we've published our 2026 annual review: The State of the Cyber Nation.

The UK faces a moment of choice. The future of cyber resilience is not set.

Read the report now:https://t.co/TzqzYedqXQ@GCHQ @DanJarvisMBE pic.twitter.com/mIFWrBB5Yt

The threat stems less from new attack techniques than from old weaknesses, NCSC chief executive Richard Horne said in prepared remarks for a speech launching the report.

"In the coming months, we face a real prospect of an unprecedented rise in the number of cyber incidents," Horne said. "But we must be clear that is not because AI has invented new ways to attack us. But because vulnerabilities that have been tolerated for far too long can now be discovered and exploited at a scale that was once unimaginable."

"For decades, we have collectively got away with doing tech on the cheap," he added.

Horne urged organizations to invest properly in their technology, stop connecting systems to the internet unnecessarily, and treat the government's Cyber Essentials certification as the starting point for their security rather than the end goal. Leaders who fail to act could have to explain to investors, staff and customers why "food isn't on shelves, lights aren't on, businesses have gone under," he warned.

The government last week announced £60 million (about $80 million) for the first phase of a national Cyber Shield, £70 million ($93 million) for a new National Exposure and Real-Time Vulnerability Centre to identify weaknesses across critical infrastructure, and a new AI Cyber Security Centre of Excellence in Manchester, run by the NCSC.

The warning follows growing evidence that attackers are already putting AI to work. In May, Google's Threat Intelligence Group said cybercriminals had used an AI model to find and weaponize a previously unknown flaw that let them bypass two-factor authentication in a popular open-source administration tool, the first time it had confirmed AI-assisted zero-day development in the wild. A Cambridge University study of more than 90,000 cybercrime forum threads published the same month, however, found that most criminals were using AI for spam and phishing rather than sophisticated attacks.

UK officials have sounded the alarm before. In July, Foreign Secretary Yvette Cooper warned that governments must agree on AI safeguards before frontier systems transform warfare, crime and society. "We cannot afford to wait for an AI equivalent of Hiroshima before we act," she wrote.

#5144. NEAR token sinks 7% as proposal to cut annual token issuance passes

The proposed issuance cut would prevent 11.8 million NEAR from entering circulation over two years, worth about $45 million at today’s price.

Near Protocol’s NEAR token fell by roughly 7% on Tuesday, despite community approval of a proposal to gradually reduce the maximum annual issuance rate from 2.5% to 1.6% over 24 months.

NEAR traded at $3.82 as of 11:06 am UTC, extending its weekly decline to roughly 13%, according to CoinMarketCap.

The latest drop followed Monday’s approval of HSP-029, which received support from 99.9% of participating voting power. About 3.28 million vote-escrowed NEAR (veNEAR) tokens were cast in favor, according to the NEAR House of Stake governance platform.

The proposal would reduce long-term token dilution while lowering projected gross staking yields from 5.2% to 3.3% by the end of the 24-month period. It estimates that issuance would fall by 11.8 million NEAR over two years and 66.1 million NEAR over six years.

The proposal remains subject to the Security Council’s 14-day veto window. If not vetoed, it will be ratified, followed by a 90-day grace period before the issuance reduction begins. Implementation also requires validators to adopt the necessary technical upgrade.

#5139. Former Sen. Sherrod Brown defeats Jon Husted, dealing a blow to crypto PAC Fairshake

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Earlier this year, Fairshake threw millions of dollars into the Ohio race, positioning itself against former Sen. Sherrod Brown.

Former Democratic Sen. Sherrod Brown is taking back his spot in the Senate, leaving crypto political action committee Fairshake with the bag as he beats out incumbent Republican Jon Husted.

On Tuesday night, Brown defeated Husted in the race for Ohio's Senate seat, dealing a setback to Fairshake, a top crypto political action committee that committed tens of millions of dollars to keep Brown out of Washington.

Earlier this year, Fairshake threw $30 million into the Ohio race, positioning itself against Brown. The crypto PAC and the broader crypto industry played a significant role in the 2024 elections, spending millions to influence political outcomes.

This is not Fairshake's first time spending against Brown. He was previously the chair of the Senate Banking Committee, where he set the agenda for crypto and called for a crackdown on the use of cryptocurrency to fund terrorism and evade sanctions.

Then in 2024, Fairshake spent $12 million to back crypto-friendly Republican Bernie Moreno against Brown, defeating him. Since Democrats regained control of the Senate, Brown could become chair of the Senate Agriculture Committee, which has jurisdiction over digital assets and prediction markets, among other things. If he is chair, sources previously told The Block that Brown would be unlikely to introduce any crypto legislation.

Sen. Husted, who is endorsed by crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto, has voted yes for a few crypto bills, including the broad crypto legislation called the Clarity Act. Husted has an A rating from the group and gave Brown an F.

Other candidates backed by Stand With Crypto such as Democrat Chris Pappas in New Hampshire won their race.

Other pivotal Senate wins include Democratic Sens. Cory Booker and Mark Warner, both of whom have been a part of the negotiations for crypto legislation. Senate Democratic candidate in Texas, James Talarico, received an A rating from crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto for his previous pro-crypto statements and his vote to create a strategic bitcoin reserve in Texas. Talarico also won his race on Tuesday against Texas Attorney General Ken Paxton.

Updated at 4:55 a.m. UTC to reflect that Democrats gained control of the Senate

#5136. French bank Crédit Mutuel Arkéa exploited for over $133 million, ZachXBT says

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

ZachXBT said he has a 'high degree of confidence' that the attacker is not associated with North Korea's Lazarus Group.

Onchain investigator ZachXBT has identified French cooperative bank Crédit Mutuel Arkéa as the alleged victim of a theft estimated at more than $133 million.

ZachXBT first reported on Nov. 2 that an unnamed EU entity had lost over $106 million from multiple centralized exchange accounts the previous day, with funds withdrawn as USDC and USDT.

A day later, he raised the total stolen amount to above $133 million after tracing an additional $27 million in withdrawals, some of which occurred after his initial disclosure. He speculated that a large financial institution in Western Europe was exploited and currently being covered up.

On the evening of Nov. 3, he stated that the institution is allegedly Crédit Mutuel Arkéa, a French bank with approximately $235 billion in assets. He noted that the attack spanned multiple days and suggested the actual losses could be higher when accounting for possible over-the-counter activity.

"Over the past 24 hours the illicit actor has been aggressively swapping USDC for ETH, transferring to instant exchanges (WellWallet, Kyrrex, etc), converting to XMR via Wagyu, and bridging across multiple chains," ZachXBT wrote in his Nov. 2 message in his Telegram group, adding that he has a "high degree of confidence" that the attacker is not associated with North Korea's Lazarus Group.

He has criticized the institution for allegedly allowing more than $30 million to be laundered, noting that proper incident disclosure to EU law enforcement could have frozen the funds.

Crédit Mutuel Arkéa has not issued any public statement confirming or denying the incident. The Block has reached out to the bank but has not heard back. The claims remain based on ZachXBT's own analysis and have not been independently verified by the bank or authorities.

The bank has more than 5.6 million customers, with total assets of 211.5 billion euros (around $237 billion).

#5135. Epidemic of Hacked Freezers Spreads Across Europe

Epidemic of Hacked Freezers Spreads Across Europe

Europe's freezers have gone rogue, with supermarkets in nine more countries reporting refrigeration systems that heated up overnight on Monday.

Stores in Belgium, Estonia, Finland, Ireland, Italy, Luxembourg, the Netherlands, Portugal and Sweden reported problems, and Estonian units were pushed to 50°C (122 °F).

No group has claimed responsibility, so the only ones taking the heat are the freezers.

The outbreak has a patient zero in France, where Finance Minister Roland Lescure told Parliament on Oct. 13 that about 700 of the country's 10,000 supermarkets were affected.

According to France's CERT-FR, hackers forced a continuous defrost cycle, which spoiled food and sparked fires at some stores. CERT-FR said it was aware of numerous compromises of remote control modules made by Danfoss and Copeland.

Groupement Mousquetaires, which owns Intermarché and Netto, counted 300 stores affected, while Cooperative U said 80 of its 2,000 shops were hit. Lescure said food supplies were not at risk but acknowledged the attack "touches the heart of our food security." Some Mousquetaires display cases were still out of service that Tuesday.

Monday's wave hit several chains in Belgium, where Carrefour reported disruption at about 15 of its 700 stores. Sabcobel, a Belgian refrigeration contractor, said evaporators had been switched into defrost mode, and federal prosecutors opened an investigation. Four Delhaize outlets in Luxembourg also reported problems.

Italy's Radenza Group, which runs Coop-branded stores in Sicily, said the attack began at 4 a.m local time. and targeted the remote-control system for frozen food sections. Ireland's Musgrave counted 13 SuperValu stores affected, and Mosqueteiros confirmed incidents at seven Intermarché stores in Portugal.

At 50°C (122 °F), a freezer is well past hot-tub territory and turns basically into a low-powered steam oven or, worse, a bacteria incubator where germs grow quickly if food is left there too long.

In Sweden, Coop said six of its roughly 800 stores were hit and that they ran on 4G connections, making them "more exposed." Ica said a limited number of stores had problems and that the affected shops used an external Danfoss control system. Neither chain has confirmed a cyberattack, and Finland's Kesko says network interference has not been ruled out.

EU Agriculture Commissioner Christophe Hansen called the incidents a "deliberate attempt" to disrupt the food supply chain. Investigators have not said whether Monday's wave is linked to the French attack.

Estonian refrigeration firm Kitman Coldtech said the affected units were older Danfoss models similar to those attacked in France. CERT-FR warns that leaving an end-of-life device without security patches exposed to the internet carries a very high risk of compromise. Researchers at Claroty published flaws in Danfoss AK-SM 800A controllers in August, including an authentication bypass.

Claroty found 2,765 of those devices exposed online, and Danfoss fixed the flaws in firmware R4.3.1. It remains unclear which weaknesses the attackers used, and CERT-FR says the campaigns are probably still ongoing, while Italy's government was set to hold a cybersecurity meeting on Tuesday.

For now, CERT-FR advises operators to take the controllers off the public internet, and Sabcobel's CEO says many older units need physical modifications that require investment. Until those arrive, there is no real fix for the oldest units, which makes your 1998 chest freezer the most secure device on the continent.

#5133. Moscow Exchange to launch Bitcoin, Ether and USDT trading in December

Moscow Exchange plans to launch crypto trading on Dec. 1 under Russia’s new law, with annual purchase limits for non-qualified investors.

Moscow Exchange plans to launch trading in Bitcoin, Ether and Tether’s USDT on Dec. 1, opening what it describes as Russia’s first organized digital currency market.

The exchange said Monday that pilot trades would begin as participants join the platform. Trades will use its existing stock-market infrastructure and be cleared through the National Clearing Center (NCC), with trading hours from 6:50 am to 11:50 pm Moscow time.

The venue will initially support same-day and next-day settlement, as well as swap transactions. Non-qualified investors must pass a test before purchasing crypto and will be limited to purchases of 300,000 rubles (about $3,700) annually per broker, according to the exchange.

The launch follows a Russian crypto law whose main provisions took effect Sept. 1. The law permits regulated trading but maintains a ban on cryptocurrency payments for goods and services in Russia.

Moscow Exchange and the NCC have been under US sanctions since June 2024, when the UK also sanctioned the exchange. The European Union added it to its sanctions list in July 2026.

Sergey Mendeleev, founder of Russian cross-border payments platform Exved, told Cointelegraph he doubted the proposed market would attract significant demand.

“Neither the global nor the local market will notice anything at all,” he said, questioning who would trade on a sanctioned exchange.

Mendeleev also predicted uncompetitive trading conditions, comparing the prospective offering to precious metals accounts at Russian lender Sberbank, where customers trade account balances without taking possession of physical metal.

Related: Russia to ban direct peer-to-peer crypto trades starting July 2027

He warned that investors could face “absolutely outrageous spreads,” referring to the difference between buying and selling prices. “For ordinary non-qualified investors, this is an entirely unattractive and unprofitable proposition,” he said.

Mendeleev saw potential for the platform to support cross-border payments. He said a venue where importers and exporters could buy and sell crypto directly with one another would be welcome, but questioned whether adding another intermediary would be competitive in a market where traders negotiate over price differences as small as 0.05%.

“I hope this will mark a genuine breakthrough for foreign trade,” he said.

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The bank is among the first to use DTCC's new tokenization service as it separately prepares to support tokenized equities in Q1 2027.

Morgan Stanley has begun using the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation's new tokenization infrastructure to tokenize real-world assets, joining a group of financial institutions that accessed the service after its launch last week.

The bank said it has tokenized U.S. equities and Treasurys through the service, making it among the first to do so, according to an X post.

#5130. Where Does Brock Purdy Live? Exploring the Homes of the San Francisco 49ers Quarterback

He spends plenty of time at Levi Stadium as the quarterback of the San Francisco 49ers, but where does Brock Purdy live when he’s off the field? The football player was born and raised in Queen Creek, Arizona, and played football at Perry High School in nearby Gilbert. While Purdy left Arizona in 2018 to play at Iowa State University, he’s still fond of his home state. “I love the state of Arizona and everyone that’s been a part of my life,” he told reporters ahead of the 2024 Super Bowl. “And obviously, I’m trying to represent them as best as I can for as long as my career goes.”

At Iowa State, Purdy was a three-time All-Big 12 quarterback and was the winningest quarterback in the school’s history. The University’s Midwest environment suited Purdy. “My favorite part is the people, just because of how down-to-earth and real everyone is,” he told the Iowa Stater in 2024. “Everyone wants to help each other out. There’s no selfishness. I don’t know what it is about the culture in Iowa, but everybody just loves one another authentically. And when you go back, you feel that every time.” Purdy also met his future wife, Jenna, his freshman year at Iowa State. The couple got engaged in 2023 and tied the knot back in Iowa in 2024.

In 2022, the San Francisco 49ers selected Purdy as the final pick of the NFL Draft. This pick typically earns the harsh nickname Mr. Irrelevant, but Purdy quickly proved the moniker wrong, becoming starting quarterback in his rookie season. Now in his fifth season with the 49ers, Purdy is also building a successful life off the field. Read on to learn about Brock Purdy’s homes over the years.

Teammates—and onetime roommates—Alfredo Gutierrez, Nick Zakelj, and Purdy in the locker room in 2022

At the beginning of his NFL career, Purdy lived more like a recent college grad than a pro football player. During his rookie year, Purdy shared a house with offensive linemen Alfredo Gutierrez and Nick Zakelj. The trio even gave out a Christmas card from the household to teammates. In an interview with NBC Sports Bay Area, Gutierrez revealed that Purdy did most of the cooking out of the three and that they spent downtime watching football, movies, and playing Mario Super Slugger.

By 2023, Purdy had only one roommate: Zakelj. “So he and I are still splitting rent,” Purdy told the TODAY Show. “I still drive my Toyota Sequoia. Other than that, it’s pretty simple.”

Purdy with his wife, Jenna, and daughter, Millie Joleen, in 2025. They now have a second child on the way.

Purdy left his shared home in 2024 when he married his wife, Jenna. They now live in a rental home in the Bay Area with their daughter, Millie Joleen, who was born in July 2025. The couple announced in July 2026 that they would soon be welcoming a second child.

The couple focused on making the home family-friendly, adding plenty of storage for their daughter’s toys, and durable furniture for hosting. “Becoming new parents, we really wanted this house to feel like a home where our family is going to continue to grow,” Jenna said in a video unveiling the couple’s living room and bedroom makeovers, which they worked on with Pottery Barn.

In May 2025, Purdy signed a five-year, $265 million deal with the 49ers, making him the fifth-highest-paid quarterback in the league. The hefty pay raise led Purdy to start his real estate portfolio. His teammate, 49ers tight end George Kittle, revealed on a podcast in June of that year that Purdy had purchased a home in Nashville.

Purdy with teammate George Kittle in 2025

“He did purchase a house in Nashville, Tennessee,” Kittle said. “He will be kind of a neighbor to me at some point. That will be pretty fun, because I’ll have a quarterback that can throw to me all the time, that’s actually on my team.” That summer, Purdy was seen training at Kittle’s football compound in Nashville.

#5129. Five Ways North Korea Breaks Into Crypto Firms—and How Binance Was Hacked

Five Ways North Korea Breaks Into Crypto Firms—and How Binance Was Hacked

Hackers from North Korea are notoriously effective at stealing crypto, with one estimate attributing 60% of all losses last year to DPRK-linked groups. These hackers enlist several different vectors of attack, making it all the more difficult to spot them in the act.

Last week, it took just 20 minutes for alleged North Korean hackers to drain $6.3 billion from Binance, in what is the biggest crypto hack on record. The attackers compromised a Binance engineer's work computer through a fake job interview, used an internal AI tool to gain control of other engineers' computers, and then used stolen credentials to hijack an internal wallet signing service, the exchange said.

In the past, North Korean hackers have been known to get hired at crypto companies under fake identities, attack through third-party infrastructure, and poison widely used code libraries. More recently, they've started using AI to make all of it more convincing.

Here are some examples of the methods that North Korean hackers are known to use in crypto attacks.

Binance chief security officer Jimmy Su told Decrypt in August 2025 that the exchange throws out suspected North Korean resumes every day, and that "almost all the large DPRK hacks," at the time, had involved a fake employee helping facilitate the attack.

Su said there were tell-tale signs in resumes that indicate the applicant was from North Korea, but the exchange was not willing to reveal the specifics on the red flags. Sometimes, though, malicious actors get through to the interview stage, where the attackers use deepfakes to change their face and voice.

"The only real good detection is that they almost always have a slow internet connection," he told Decrypt. "What's happening is that the translation and the voice changer are working during the call … that's why they are always delayed."

Those who do get hired tend to be suspiciously good at the job. Su said North Korean workers are often among a company's top performers, likely because several people may be sharing one role across time zones.

The threat has not let up. Binance said its security team detected and prevented 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threat cases in 2026, using techniques ranging from fraudulent job interviews to fake video conferencing apps.

Attackers also flip the scam around and pose as recruiters to target people who already work at high-profile crypto companies. Often, the attacker will claim there's a video or audio issue during the interview and send the victim a link to update Zoom, which infects their device with malware

"The DPRK group will [also] try to schedule calls with the external-facing employees," Su told Decrypt. "Either as a DeFi project or investment firm. Worst yet, they'll be recruiting them for a high-level job, paying twice, three times as much, just to get them onto an interview."

This is how the Binance attack began, according to the exchange. In September, a Binance software engineer was approached through a legitimate job-hunting platform by someone posing as a project director, who set up two interviews. In the first, a purported HR representative built trust over a live video call by sharing a social media account full of personal photos and videos, an approach Binance called "more advanced and deceptive than previously seen."

In the second, held on a chat app, the engineer was told to download an "assignment" onto his work computer. The code repository contained previously unknown malware that ran during setup, swapped a legitimate developer tool for a malicious version and connected to an attacker-controlled server, evading Binance's security tools.

One North Korea-linked group infected at least 30,000 devices in more than 100 countries between roughly December 2025 and July 2026 using similar methods. It took funds or credentials from more than 7,000 crypto wallets and moved around $10.71 million to North Korea.

The group, known as WaterPlum or Contagious Interview, posed as AI, crypto, or NFT companies and set candidates a technical interview or coding task that required downloading malware-filled files. Some used AI face-swapping software during interviews before cutting their video and asking the candidate to do the same, blaming a bad connection.

Binance's Su also told Decrypt that North Korean hackers poison public code libraries, ranking it as the second most common method of attack—behind fake employees.

Node Package Manager (NPM) packages are collections of reusable, open-source code that developers frequently use. Attackers can clone these packages and slip in a small line of malicious code while keeping its original function. That means that a victim could use this poisoned code library and not notice, with the malicious code embedding itself deeper over time as more is built on top of it.

In September 2025, software security firm ReversingLabs found two NPM packages that used Ethereum smart contracts to download malware, which the firm said it hadn't seen before. The packages were tied to a wider network of GitHub repositories, mostly branded as crypto trading bots, with thousands of commits and stars that ReversingLabs thinks were mostly faked.

The tactic formed part of a “sophisticated campaign” of malicious actors attempting to hack users via poisoned blockchain-related public code libraries, a vector of attack that Binance has previously attributed to North Korean hackers.

To counter the threat, Su said, Binance makes employees go through NPM libraries with a fine-tooth comb.

Tampered developer tools also played a part in the Binance hack. In June, a member of Binance's wallet DevOps team downloaded a tampered installation of Homebrew, a popular software installer, while trying to install an AI coding tool he had found through a search engine, the exchange said. Its security team caught the problem, replaced the device and rotated his credentials, but the incident exposed the engineer and his colleagues on the wallet infrastructure team to the attackers.

Before Binance, the unwanted record for the largest crypto hack belonged to Bybit, which lost $1.4 billion in February 2025. Cybersecurity firms revealed that North Korean hackers pulled off the attack by planting malicious code into the infrastructure of Safe—a crypto wallet provider used by the exchange.

The security firms said malicious JavaScript code was directly injected into Safe's online infrastructure. Though, it was unclear how the attackers managed to do this.

Bybit's infrastructure "was not compromised," the exchange told Decrypt, and the hack came purely because a third party was exploited.

The Binance attack targeted the exchange's own systems. On October 29, the attacker used stolen credentials to deploy a container and gain unauthorized access to an internal wallet signing service, which let it initiate transactions outside Binance's normal withdrawal process. On Ethereum, it used the signing service to exploit EIP-7702, a mechanism that lets an account delegate execution rights to a smart contract, giving an attacker-controlled contract partial control over a Binance hot wallet.

Those on-chain authorizations kept working even after Binance suspended withdrawals. Because the hot wallet used multi-party computation, there was no single private key Binance could use to revoke them quickly, the exchange said.

Every single one of the above mentioned attack vectors can be turbo-charged with artificial intelligence tools.

Early this year, Google warned of an AI-powered malware campaign targeting crypto companies coming from North Korea. Similarly, Binance's Su also mentioned that AI tools can be used to increase the effectiveness of attacks.

Concerns around AI-powered hacks have risen in recent months, with Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin saying in October that it's "plausible" AI could break encryption within the next two years. It came amid an explosion in AI-driven maths, with OpenAI claiming to have cracked a 90-year-old maths problem in a matter of days. Experts urged the industry to prepare for AI-powered attacks on encryption.

There are no confirmed reports of North Korean hackers using AI to break encryption. But given how quickly they've adopted AI elsewhere, the industry may have to prepare for that next.

#5127. DeFi Explained

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Understanding How Decentralized Finance Protocols Work

DeFi, or decentralized finance, works through smart contracts that coordinate financial activity on-chain. Instead of relying on a centralized intermediary, DeFi protocols use liquidity pools, automated market makers, collateral rules, oracles, governance systems, and composable protocol integrations to support trading, lending, yield strategies, derivatives, and other financial functions.

DeFi 201 is intended for readers who already understand crypto basics and want to understand how DeFi protocols actually work. It moves beyond beginner definitions and into the systems that keep decentralized finance running: exchanges, lending markets, yield strategies, oracle networks, derivatives, governance, and composability.

DeFi is best understood as a connected protocol system rather than a list of isolated apps. Its main components include decentralized exchanges, lending protocols, yield aggregators, oracles, decentralized derivatives, governance, and composability.

how decentralized exchanges enable on-chain trading through order books, AMMs, liquidity pools, and intent-based execution;

how DeFi lending markets use collateral, pooled liquidity, utilization, interest rates, and liquidations;

how yield aggregators use vaults, strategy routing, managers, and keepers to automate DeFi strategies;

why oracles are critical for prices, collateral values, liquidations, derivatives, and synthetic assets;

how decentralized derivatives create synthetic exposure through collateral, margin, funding, and liquidation mechanics;

how DAOs and governance tokens coordinate proposals, voting, delegation, treasuries, and protocol upgrades;

how composability lets protocols build on each other while creating dependency and systemic-risk trade-offs.

Explain how DEX architectures route trades and allocate liquidity.

Describe the mechanics and risks of DeFi lending protocols.

Explain the role of vaults and automated yield strategies.

Identify oracle dependencies and manipulation risk.

Describe how DeFi protocols package derivative exposure without centralized intermediaries.

Explain how DAOs coordinate protocol upgrades and incentives.

Analyze both the benefits and risks of DeFi composability.

The lesson summaries below are a short overview of the full course content. Take the full interactive DeFi 201 course to explore each lesson in more depth through guided explanations, examples, and activities.

Compares on-chain order books, constant-product, concentrated-liquidity, constant-sum, and hybrid AMMs with intent-based routing by competing solvers.

Follows collateralized borrowing through pooled liquidity, utilization-based rates, supply and borrow APY, health factors, and liquidation mechanics.

Shows how vaults route deposits across strategies and automate compounding while adding smart-contract, strategy, fee, governance, and automation risk.

Explains how smart contracts obtain price and other external data and why source quality, aggregation, latency, manipulation resistance, and failure modes matter across DeFi.

Introduces perpetuals, options, and synthetic assets and connects their design to collateral, margin, funding, liquidation, oracle, and leverage risks.

Covers governance tokens, proposals, delegation, quorum, voting power, treasuries, protocol upgrades, and the risks of low participation or concentrated control.

Explains how protocols combine as money legos to create new products while dependencies can transmit smart-contract, oracle, collateral, bridge, or governance failures across DeFi.

DeFi means decentralized finance. It refers to financial protocols that run through blockchains and smart contracts rather than traditional intermediaries. In DeFi, users can trade, lend, borrow, supply liquidity, manage collateral, vote on protocol changes, or build products by connecting multiple protocols together.

The important idea is not that DeFi removes every kind of intermediation. It changes where coordination happens. Rules that would usually be enforced by a platform, broker, exchange, or bank can be encoded into smart contracts, governance systems, oracle feeds, and liquidity mechanisms.

Most DeFi protocols combine a few common building blocks: users, smart contracts, liquidity providers, traders, borrowers, lenders, governance participants, and external data providers. The exact mix depends on the protocol type.

Smart contracts: Encode the rules for trading, lending, collateral, fees, voting, or settlement.

Liquidity pools: Hold assets that traders or borrowers interact with.

Liquidity providers: Supply assets to pools and take protocol-specific risks.

Oracles: Bring external data, such as prices, into smart contracts.

Governance: Coordinates upgrades, parameters, incentives, and treasury decisions.

Composability: Lets protocols plug into each other and create new products.

Decentralized exchanges allow users to trade on-chain through smart contracts. Early exchange design borrowed from order books, where buyers and sellers post bids and asks. But on-chain order books can be expensive and capital-inefficient because each order update consumes gas and liquidity may sit idle.

Automated market makers changed that model. In an AMM, traders do not need to match with another person’s order. They trade against a shared liquidity pool supplied by liquidity providers. A liquidity pool is a smart contract that holds tokens, and an algorithmic formula determines prices based on the pool’s reserves.

The constant product AMM model uses the formula x times y equals k. It keeps the product of the two token reserves constant, so prices move automatically as traders buy one asset and add another. This model is simple and resilient, but it spreads liquidity across all possible prices, which can make capital inefficient.

Core idea: Matches bids and asks directly.

Trade-off: Costly and capital-inefficient on-chain.

Strength: Simple, always provides liquidity.

Trade-off: Capital may sit unused far from market price.

Strength: Higher capital efficiency and tighter spreads.

Trade-off: Requires more active liquidity management.

Core idea: Keeps exchange rate near fixed parity.

Trade-off: Can break if one side of the pool is drained.

Core idea: Blends product and sum curves.

Strength: Useful for stable or pegged assets.

Trade-off: More complex and less intuitive.

Core idea: Users express desired outcomes; solvers compete to fulfill them.

Strength: Can improve routing and user experience.

Trade-off: Depends on solver competition and execution quality.

Liquidity providers deposit assets into pools so other users can trade. In return, LPs may earn a share of trading fees. But providing liquidity is not passive income without risk. LP returns depend on trading volume, fee tiers, price movement, pool design, and impermanent loss.

LP profitability can be expressed as trading fees earned minus impermanent loss, if any.

DeFi lending protocols let users supply assets to earn yield and borrow against collateral without a traditional bank. Smart contracts replace bilateral lending relationships with pooled markets that set rules, manage collateral, and enforce liquidations automatically.

A typical lending protocol uses overcollateralization. A borrower deposits collateral, borrows a smaller value against it, and must keep the position healthy. If collateral value falls too far, the protocol can liquidate the position to protect the lending pool.

Collateral: Assets pledged to secure a loan or leveraged position.

Utilization: How much of a lending pool is currently borrowed.

Supply APY: The rate suppliers may earn for lending assets.

Borrow APY: The rate borrowers pay to use borrowed assets.

Health factor: A measure of whether a position is safely collateralized.

Liquidation: Forced sale or closure when a position becomes unsafe.

Yield aggregators simplify DeFi yield generation by routing deposits across strategies and automating parts of the process. Instead of manually moving funds between pools or protocols, users deposit into a vault. The aggregator then follows a strategy, compounds returns, and abstracts away some of the operational work.

This does not remove risk. Aggregators add another layer of smart-contract exposure, strategy risk, fee policy, governance risk, and dependence on keepers or automation agents. Yield optimization is a mechanism to understand, not a recommendation.

DeFi protocols often need information that does not naturally live on-chain. Lending markets need asset prices to value collateral. Derivatives need reference prices. Synthetic assets need external data. Liquidations depend on timely and accurate price information.

Oracles provide this data to smart contracts. Because smart contracts can execute automatically based on oracle inputs, oracle design is a core security issue. Bad data, delayed data, manipulation, or concentrated data sources can create losses across trading, lending, derivatives, and liquidation systems.

More resilient designs combine multiple sources and aggregate observations before publishing a value on-chain. That can reduce dependence on one provider, but it does not eliminate latency, source correlation, or governance risk.

Decentralized derivatives create exposure to assets or market outcomes without necessarily requiring users to hold the underlying asset directly. Common examples include perpetual futures, options, synthetic assets, and other collateralized instruments.

The key mechanics include collateral, margin, funding rates, liquidation thresholds, and protocol risk controls. Perpetual futures have no expiry and use funding payments to help keep contract prices aligned with spot prices. Like other DeFi products, derivatives can increase capital efficiency but also introduce leverage, liquidation, oracle, and systemic-risk concerns.

Many DeFi protocols are governed through DAOs, governance tokens, proposals, voting systems, delegation, and treasuries. Governance determines how protocols update parameters, allocate incentives, manage upgrades, and respond to risk.

Proposals may need a quorum or other participation threshold before a vote can take effect. The exact rules determine how voting power is counted, how delegates act, and how quickly a protocol can respond to changing conditions.

Governance can make protocols more community-driven, but it can also create concentration and coordination problems. Token-weighted voting may give large holders outsized influence. Low voter participation can make decisions fragile. Delegation can improve expertise but may also centralize practical control.

Composability is the ability for DeFi protocols to plug into and build on top of one another. This is why DeFi is often described as money legos. A lending protocol can use an oracle, a yield aggregator can route deposits across lending markets, and a derivatives protocol can depend on multiple liquidity and data systems.

Composability accelerates innovation because developers can reuse existing liquidity, infrastructure, and protocol primitives. But it also creates dependency risk. If one widely used protocol, oracle, bridge, collateral asset, or governance decision fails, the effects can cascade across many connected applications.

What it does: Enables on-chain trading through pools, curves, routing, or intents.

Main risk: Slippage, LP risk, smart-contract risk, solver/routing risk.

What it does: Lets users supply assets and borrow against collateral.

Main risk: Liquidation, collateral volatility, oracle risk.

What it does: Automates deposits, routing, strategies, and compounding.

Main risk: Strategy risk, contract risk, fee/governance risk.

What it does: Supplies external data to smart contracts.

Main risk: Data manipulation, latency, source concentration.

What it does: Creates synthetic or leveraged exposure.

Main risk: Funding, liquidation, margin, oracle, and leverage risk.

What it does: Coordinates protocol decisions and upgrades.

Main risk: Voter concentration, low participation, governance capture.

What it does: Connects multiple protocols into new products.

Main risk: Dependency and cascading failure risk.

DeFi replaces some intermediaries with code, liquidity systems, and governance. That does not remove risk. It changes the form of risk.

Smart contract risk: code can contain bugs or be exploited.

Oracle risk: bad or delayed data can trigger wrong pricing, lending, or liquidation outcomes.

Liquidity risk: pools can become shallow, imbalanced, or expensive to trade against.

Liquidation risk: collateralized positions can be closed when markets move quickly.

Governance risk: token voting can be captured, delayed, or dominated by large holders.

Composability risk: protocols that depend on one another can transmit failures across the system.

Regulatory risk: DeFi protocols, interfaces, and participants may face changing legal treatment.

This page is for educational purposes and explains protocol mechanics. It does not provide investment, trading, tax, or legal advice.

A user wants to trade ETH for USDC through a decentralized exchange. Instead of matching with a specific seller, the user trades against an ETH/USDC liquidity pool. Liquidity providers have deposited both assets into the pool. The smart contract calculates the trade based on the pool’s pricing formula, updates the token reserves, charges a trading fee, and sends the output token to the user.

The user gets an on-chain swap. LPs earn fees. The pool’s price changes because its reserves changed. If the trade is large relative to pool liquidity, the user may experience slippage. If the pool is part of a larger aggregator or intent-based route, solvers or routers may search across multiple liquidity sources to improve execution.

DeFi protocols use smart contracts to coordinate financial activity without a traditional centralized intermediary.

DEXs and AMMs use liquidity pools and algorithmic pricing to support on-chain trading.

DeFi lending protocols use collateral, utilization, rates, and liquidations to manage risk.

Yield aggregators automate strategy routing and compounding, but they add another layer of protocol risk.

Oracles are critical because many DeFi systems depend on accurate external data.

Decentralized derivatives introduce collateral, margin, funding, leverage, and liquidation dynamics.

Governance and composability are major strengths of DeFi, but they also create coordination and systemic-risk trade-offs.

This is an intermediate topic. Helpful background knowledge includes blockchain basics, Ethereum basics, wallets, tokens, smart contracts, basic trading terms, and basic lending vocabulary.

Related Campus topics include Ethereum & Layer 1s 201 for smart contracts, gas, and token standards; Market Structure 201 for liquidity, order books, derivatives, and institutions; Scaling & Layer 2s 201 for cheaper execution environments and rollup context; and stablecoins, RWAs, and glossary pages for collateral, settlement, and liquidity context.

DEX: A decentralized exchange that lets users trade through smart contracts rather than a centralized intermediary.

AMM: An automated market maker that prices trades algorithmically using pooled liquidity.

Liquidity pool: A smart contract holding tokens supplied by liquidity providers for traders to swap against.

Liquidity provider: A user or entity that deposits assets into a pool and earns fees while taking pool risk.

Constant product AMM: An AMM model that preserves the invariant x times y equals k.

Concentrated liquidity: A liquidity design that lets LPs provide capital inside selected price ranges.

Intent-based DEX: A DEX model where users express desired outcomes and solvers compete to execute them.

Collateral: Assets pledged to secure a DeFi loan or leveraged position.

Liquidation: The forced closing or sale of collateral when a position becomes unsafe.

Yield aggregator: A protocol that routes funds across strategies to optimize yield.

Oracle: A service that provides external data, such as prices, to smart contracts.

Perpetual futures: Derivative contracts with no expiry that use funding rates to track spot prices.

DAO: A decentralized organization governed through proposals, votes, and smart-contract rules.

Governance token: A token used to vote on protocol decisions or delegate voting power.

Composability: The ability for protocols to plug into and build on top of one another.

DeFi works through smart contracts that coordinate activity such as trading, lending, collateral management, yield strategies, derivatives, and governance on-chain.

An AMM is an automated market maker. It lets traders swap tokens against a liquidity pool, with prices set by an algorithmic formula rather than a traditional order book.

DeFi lending protocols let users supply assets to earn yield or borrow against collateral through smart-contract-based lending markets.

Yield aggregators are protocols that route deposits across strategies and automate parts of yield generation, such as strategy selection and compounding.

DeFi protocols need oracles because many smart contracts depend on external data, especially asset prices used for collateral values, derivatives, and liquidations.

#5125. Elon Musk’s Tesla and SpaceX Block Their Stock From OKX-ICE Tokenized Venue

Elon Musk’s Tesla and SpaceX Block Their Stock From OKX-ICE Tokenized Venue

Two of Elon Musk’s companies are now keeping their shares off OKX and ICE’s planned tokenized stock venue.

Tesla and SpaceX are among six new objecting issuers listed in an updated notice from OKXICE TSV dated Monday. Amazon, Netflix, Lam Research, and SoFi Technologies are the others. The additions bring the total to 56 companies.

The list stood at 50 in the venue’s Oct. 30 notice, when Nvidia, Costco, and Nike led the biggest batch yet. That followed 38 on Oct. 28, after Walmart joined, and nine on Oct. 15, when the first objectors appeared.

The new names deepen the roster of megacap holdouts. Amazon, Tesla, and Netflix join Microsoft, Alphabet, and Nvidia, while SoFi, a fintech company that offers crypto trading, adds a company with its own stake in digital assets. SpaceX, which trades under the ticker SPCX, rounds out the batch.

Under the SEC’s innovation exemption, which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate, a venue must notify a company before offering a version of its shares tokenized by an unaffiliated third party.

If the company objects in writing within 30 days, the venue can’t make that stock available for trading, according to the SEC’s order. The objection applies only to that venue.

OKXICE TSV, a 50-50 joint venture between NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange and OKX’s U.S. holding company, plans to offer tokenized U.S. stocks 24/7 through permissioned Uniswap v4 pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain, paired with stablecoins such as USDC.

To trade, users must pass identity and sanctions checks and hold a nontransferable “soulbound” token that the venue’s smart contracts verify before every transaction.

Apple is now one of the few megacap names still missing from the list. And while objections bar OKXICE from listing third-party tokenized versions of a company’s stock, they don’t close every door.

The notice says the venue can also list shares tokenized by the issuers themselves, though it doesn’t identify any, and that tokenized stocks it offers aren’t restricted to trading on OKXICE alone.
