#5089. 11 Christmas Centerpiece Ideas That Are Fa-la-la-la-bulous

A festive Christmas centerpiece is not just a decorative focal point, it also sets the scene for making memories around the table. Whether you dream up a candy cane–inspired color scheme with elegant white china and poinsettia-red floral arrangements, or you create a winter tablescape complete with snowflakes, snowmen, and flickering tea lights, a holiday table is the perfect opportunity to get creative and channel your yuletide style. And the good news is, a holiday centerpiece isn’t just for a dining table—kitchen islands, foyers, and coffee tables all lend themselves well to a festive display. To inspire you, we’ve gathered our favorite Christmas centerpiece ideas that include everything from a classic cranberry-red palette to a designer’s chic jewel-toned Christmas table.

If you’re going for understated elegance, take cues from this Christmas table. Layer together natural elements like foraged foliage, evergreen branches, pinecones, and antlers, then add height with candleholders. “The palette is deliberately restrained and a departure from traditional holiday decor to maintain elegance and elevate the materials,” says Rae McCalmont of PBF Homes. The Christmas decor leans into deep forest greens, warm walnut tones, soft ivory, and earthy neutrals. “Layered greenery spills organically across the table to keep the presentation soft and unfussy, metallic hints, a mix of etched and colored glassware, and eclectic dinnerware elevate the presentation, while softly glowing candlelight introduces warmth,” McCalmont says.

“When planning for my Christmas dining table centerpiece, I found these fabulous candlesticks in amber, peach, and gold to complement my dining room wall mural without screaming ‘red and green’ like the typical holiday colors,” says Maggie Griffin, founder and lead designer at Maggie Griffin Design. She arranged the collection of candlesticks with white tapers down the center of the table, while “touches of jewel tones in the glassware and table linens add to the festive vibe and layered tabletop,” Griffin shares.

Pack a punch with a large Christmas arrangement that adds a festive pop of color. When assembling a seasonal DIY centerpiece, consider the vessel as an integral part of the arrangement. Here, Griffin layered seasonal magnolia and bright red berries in deep coral and celadon jars that she keeps on the kitchen counter year-round. “It’s a nod to the holiday season without the more traditional color scheme,” she says. “I believe in enhancing your home for the holidays, not totally redecorating it,” Griffin adds.

“We wanted the holiday decor to feel like a natural extension of the room rather than introduce an entirely new Christmas palette,” says Lindsey Herod, owner and principal designer at Lindsey Herod Interiors. The designer believes a room’s color story is a great starting point for any holiday table centerpiece. “Here, the centerpiece and holiday greenery can be lush and romantic because they’re speaking the same language as the chintz, antiques, and existing palette; it feels festive, but still completely part of the house,” Herod explains.

“It can be fun to look beyond traditional holiday themes and embrace decor that evokes the sense of magic and wonder that the season is all about,” says interior designer Bethany Adams, founder of Bethany Adams Interiors. For this centerpiece inspired by her late grandmother’s favorite china pattern, Adams wanted to create a quiet woodland that the deer on the mantel might have just visited. “A soft bed of moss, mushrooms of various sizes, and elegant taper candles lead the eye toward the mantel and its exuberant display of greenery,” she says. The best part? “Without obvious holiday themes, this centerpiece works all the way through the New Year,” Adams says.

Add a festive touch to a holiday party table or kitchen island with a custom gingerbread house. “We designed the gingerbread house as a playful nod to the home’s architecture, complete with an illuminated interior that echoes its expansive front entry windows,” says Kristi Will, founder of Kristi Will Interior Design. To make it extra special, the gingerbread house was personalized with the family’s dogs at the front door to welcome guests. “Created as the centerpiece for their holiday party, it brought a sense of whimsy and personality to the heart of the home,” Will says.

Embrace Christmas nostalgia by turning a vintage tabletop tree into a charming holiday centerpiece. Interior designer Bambi A’Lynn Bratton, founder of Bambi A’Lynn Interior Design, has a white Christmas tree, so this vintage German tree was the ideal choice for her table. “I have collected antique German ornaments for years and needed a special place to display them, so when I came across this little tree I knew it was the perfect fit,” she says. “I have also used this piece on a coffee table when I have a dinner party during the holidays, or on the hearth of a fireplace on top of a stack of books,” the designer shares.

For an elegant holiday table, you can’t go wrong with floral arrangements in classic Christmas red. “When it comes to styling the floral arrangements, I always make sure the florals are low enough so everyone at the table can see each other easily, but still bountiful enough so everyone can admire the gorgeous flowers used,” says interior stylist Carolyne Steiner of The Carolyne Collective. To complete the holiday tablescape, Steiner dotted white candlesticks in between the florals and placed ornaments on the plates. “I collect ornaments throughout the year for each guest invited to my event—it serves as their place card,” she shares. “Before we sit down for our meal, each person has to guess which place setting is theirs; it ends up becoming a really fun game, conversation piece, and icebreaker before dinner begins,” Steiner says.

Use a potted white orchid as an elegant Christmas centerpiece on a foyer table for a festive welcome. “The white orchid is just a lovely neutral decor item that adds some formality to a space,” says Grace Brackman, founder and principal designer at Grace Brackman Design. She notes that white orchids look particularly beautiful paired with more traditional decor. “Add some interest by potting it in a pretty ceramic bowl enhanced with some fresh-cut magnolia and fir,” Brackman suggests.

When creating a Christmas centerpiece, think beyond the surface of the dining table. “Decorating the chandelier draws the eye upward and makes the entire table feel more festive, rather than limiting the holiday elements to the tablescape itself,” says Lauren Sullivan, founder and curator at Well x Design. Here, red ribbon on the chandelier echoes the ribbon on the Christmas wreaths that hang from each chair, and visually extends the centerpiece beyond the table. “It also helps connect the table to the rest of the room and makes the whole setting feel more complete,” Sullivan says.

Arrange mercury glass trees and votive holders down the center of the dinner table to add height and create a magical ambience. “The reflective qualities of mercury glass make it a great choice for a candlelight dinner—it sets the mood and makes it feel extra festive,” Brackman says. To create an organic table runner and a soft backdrop of color for the trees to sit on, the designer recommends piling fresh-cut greenery at the base of the mercury pieces. “In my opinion, live greenery is a necessity as nothing beats the smell of fresh-cut pine,” Brackman states.

#5088. Wall Street killed Bitcoin’s wild rallies: 10x’s Markus Thielen

Markus Thielen sees Bitcoin reaching $90,000 by year-end, questions institutional ETF demand and says Ethereum must do more for holders.

Bitcoin’s days of consecutive double-digit price surges are over as Wall Street traders and profit-taking whales reshape the market, according to 10x Research founder Markus Thielen.

Institutional traders selling call options after rallies have made Bitcoin’s violent price explosions of earlier years less common, Thielen says in the latest episode of Cointelegraph’s Trade Secrets.

Thielen is also the author of Crypto Titans, a history of the industry. Writing the book led him to notice how each cycle brought different people pitching crypto to investors.

“Every cycle had actually different promoters, and different promoters bring in different investors,” he says.

This time, the promoters come from Wall Street, where investors trade on economic data and Federal Reserve decisions. Thielen says he expects Bitcoin will rise quickly on news, then spend time crab walking across the chart before the next big macro event.

Thielen estimates that market makers account for up to half of the $60 billion in net spot Bitcoin ETF inflows he analyzed. Those firms hold ETF shares as a way to hedge their options trades, so the positions do not necessarily represent a bet that Bitcoin’s price will rise.

“So in the end it was not outright buying,” he says.

His estimate draws on a review of Form 13F filings, which disclose certain securities holdings of investment managers.

Cumulative net inflows into US spot Bitcoin ETFs. Source: Coinglass

Thielen says the first wave of funds into the ETFs also included retail investors buying through institutions and corporate vehicles. Some paid substantial premiums to the value of the underlying crypto holdings.

“I would still think the institutions could come. I would still think that some of the sovereign wealth funds could come,” Thielen says.

Thielen still sees Bitcoin reaching $90,000 by year-end. He says a drop into the mid-to-high $70,000s could come first.

Bitcoin has already been trading in that range, falling to about $76,100 on Friday following Binance’s $6.3 billion hack. BTC finished October down 7.3%, according to Coinglass.

Thielen compares the recovery in prices over recent months with early 2023, when Bitcoin rose from around $20,000 to $30,000. He says the doubts surrounding this year’s rebound are familiar as investors scan for the next price drop and entry point.

BTC’s return to $90,000 may rest partly on how much further the Fed raises borrowing rates. The central bank increased its benchmark rate by a quarter of a percentage point in September, to a target range of 3.75%–4%.

It held rates steady last Wednesday in a split vote, with two officials voting for another quarter-point increase. The Fed’s statement said inflation was still elevated, leaving open the possibility of further increases.

Thielen expects another hike by December, followed by a more cautious approach to further increases that could help Bitcoin rally toward year-end.

Thielen expects Bitcoin to outperform Ether, saying too much of the value from Ethereum activity goes to layer-2 networks.

Making those networks cheaper to use has been a deliberate part of Ethereum’s development. But cheaper transactions may muddy the investment pitch for ETH.

Ethereum burns base fees paid by users, removing those coins from circulation. Its supply shrinks when the amount burned exceeds new ETH issued to validators. Lower fees can mean less ETH is destroyed.

ETH supply grows when issuance exceeds the amount burned. Source: ultrasound.money

Thielen, who says he has been bearish on Ether for more than a year, compares Ethereum with a highway.

“Everybody wants the highway to be empty so you can go fast, and the roads need to be super, super clean and fixed and everything, but nobody wants to pay for it,” he says.

He singles out Hyperliquid as an example of how trading activity can fund token purchases. The decentralized perpetuals exchange’s assistance fund converts trading fees into HYPE, which is then burned.

For Ethereum, he wants a stronger connection between the network’s use and the financial benefit to holders.

Magazine: Bitcoin turns 18: Has the original rebel grown up?

#5087. CrowdStrike Launches AI Agent 'Kill Switch,' Plans to Acquire Fiddler AI

CrowdStrike Launches AI Agent 'Kill Switch,' Plans to Acquire Fiddler AI

CrowdStrike has introduced an AI agent kill switch designed to stop unauthorized activity inside corporate networks, alongside plans to acquire AI observability startup Fiddler as businesses give increasingly autonomous software access to their systems.

The cybersecurity company announced the launch Tuesday at its Fal.Con Europe 2026 conference in Barcelona, expanding its Falcon Guardian security platform with controls that let administrators intervene when AI agents stray beyond their assigned tasks.

The move tackles a problem companies face as they deploy AI systems capable of executing code, accessing sensitive information, and interacting with business applications. Unlike conventional chatbots, these agents can perform sequences of actions with limited human oversight, potentially causing damage even when nobody has deliberately attacked them.

In practice, an agent authorized to organize corporate files might misinterpret its instructions, follow malicious directions embedded in a document, or execute a command its operator never intended. The same access that makes autonomous agents useful can also make their mistakes expensive.

The new AI Kill Switch gives security administrators a way to interrupt an agent's activity without waiting for it to complete its task. According to CrowdStrike's technical explanation, the currently available mechanism works through AI gateways, which can block an agent's access to the models powering its decisions.

The initial release does not yet provide every type of containment CrowdStrike has described. The company plans to expand the feature to terminate agent processes directly on endpoints and coordinate restrictions across cloud environments and other enterprise systems.

An authorized administrator triggers the kill switch, and the action is recorded in an audit trail. CrowdStrike also describes controls to prevent terminated agents from restarting and restrict their network access, with broader enforcement capabilities still under development.

The company is adding protections at other points where agents operate. Falcon Guardian can inspect agent prompts and apply policies to tools including Claude Code, OpenAI Codex, GitHub Copilot, Microsoft 365 Copilot, Cursor, and OpenCode.

The risk is already well documented. As Decrypt previously reported, Microsoft researchers identified a vulnerability in Anthropic's Claude Code GitHub Action that could have allowed attackers to manipulate the coding agent into exposing sensitive credentials through malicious repository content. Anthropic subsequently patched the flaw but Crowdstrike’s solution would be more extensive.

CrowdStrike also signed an agreement to acquire Fiddler AI, a company specializing in monitoring AI models and explaining their behavior.

The deal values Fiddler at roughly $400 million, according to The Wall Street Journal. CrowdStrike has not publicly disclosed the acquisition price, although it said the transaction would be paid primarily in cash, with some stock subject to vesting conditions.

Fiddler's technology addresses a related problem that a kill switch alone cannot solve: understanding what an AI agent was doing before something went wrong, and whether its actions were appropriate in the first place.

Its software evaluates agents before deployment, traces their decisions and tool calls, and monitors changes in behavior after they begin operating. These records can help security teams investigate unexpected actions or provide evidence to auditors.

CrowdStrike expects to complete the Fiddler acquisition during the fourth quarter of fiscal 2027, which ends January 31, 2027, subject to customary closing conditions. Its expanded endpoint kill-switch capabilities have not yet received a specific release date.

#5083. Anthropic's AI Is Getting Cheaper to Use. That's Becoming a Problem for Its Business

Anthropic's AI Is Getting Cheaper to Use. That's Becoming a Problem for Its Business

Anthropic is making Claude cheaper to use. Its computing bill, however, is moving in the other direction.

Anthropic said revenue growth from increased usage was "partially offset by a decrease of approximately $600 million from price reductions introduced as part of new model versions and contractual discounts," according to its amended SEC filing.

Meanwhile, compute and infrastructure expenses surged from $5.56 billion to $9.86 billion, an increase of roughly 77%.

That means Anthropic spent roughly 58 cents on compute and infrastructure for every dollar of revenue, versus 48 cents a quarter earlier. The measure includes computing used for research, training, and internal operations—not just serving paying Claude customers—so it isn't a per-prompt profit margin.

The $600 million figure isn't revenue that vanished from Anthropic's books. It's the amount the company says price reductions and discounts offset revenue growth driven by higher usage.

The cuts are not hypothetical. Claude Opus 5.5, which lowered token prices compared with Opus 5, was recently released, being more capable than any previous model, and sometimes even up to par against Fable. Anthropic also introduced Haiku 5.5 in October, pitching a much cheaper model for high-volume work.

Anthropic says Fable 5.1 reduced the cost of typical workloads by around 25%, while Opus 5.5 brought customers' cost per task down roughly 40% compared with Opus 5. The reductions reflect both lower prices and efficiency gains, rather than straight discounts across every task. Anthropic detailed Fable's lower cache-read pricing in its model announcement.

There is a business case for that strategy: cheaper, more capable AI can encourage customers to use more of it. Anthropic says users have historically expanded consumption after such releases.

But its latest quarter also shows how difficult it is to synchronize demand with supply. Anthropic typically reserves compute capacity 12 to 18 months before it becomes available, leaving much of that spending relatively fixed in the near term. It’s also infamous among AI enthusiasts because its quotas tend to deplete too easily.

The quarter included $5 billion of compute under short-term commitments that Anthropic says cost significantly more than comparable capacity secured through multiyear contracts. That expense helped push the company from a $974 million operating profit in the second quarter to a $567 million operating loss in the third.

The longer-term stakes are bigger. Anthropic's IPO filing, means the company is preparing to go public, and put the company in Wall Street’s bulls eye. Its updated disclosures put aggregate lease and purchasing commitments at roughly $895.4 billion, substantially all related to compute and infrastructure.

Reuters has also reported that the filing raised questions about whether infrastructure partners can secure the financing needed to deliver all that capacity. As of now, Google and Nvidia seem to be the most benefited from Anthropic’s growth as its core business depends on their infrastructure.

For now, demand for Claude is still growing rapidly. The harder question is whether Anthropic can keep making its models cheaper faster than the cost of building and running them comes down. Right now, OpenAI, which is also preparing its own IPO offers a competitive model, with many usage resets, and a more generous usage quota.

That’s the main issue keeping Anthropic from rising prices.

#5080. Tether Holds 150,000 Bitcoin, CEO Says—About 50,000 More Than Its Reserve Report Shows

Tether Holds 150,000 Bitcoin, CEO Says—About 50,000 More Than Its Reserve Report Shows

Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino said the stablecoin giant now holds around 150,000 Bitcoin, worth roughly $11.5 billion at current prices, a figure that exceeds what the company’s own reserve disclosures imply by some 50,000 coins.

“We have give or take 150,000 bitcoin ourselves. It’s not much, but it’s honest work,” Ardoino said during a keynote at the Plan ₿ Forum in Lugano, Switzerland, the city where Tether has run a Bitcoin and stablecoin adoption program since 2022.

The claim is notable because it doesn’t square neatly with the paper trail. Tether’s third-quarter reserve attestation, which covers the assets backing its USDT stablecoin, lists $8.37 billion of Bitcoin valued at $83,621 per coin, implying roughly 100,060 BTC.

That leaves about 50,000 coins, worth close to $3.8 billion, that its public filings do not yet account for. Ardoino did not say where the additional Bitcoin is held or when it was acquired, so his comment doesn’t indicate a fresh purchase of that size, and Tether has long maintained that its corporate Bitcoin investments sit separately from the reserves supporting USDT.

The stablecoin issuer has been accumulating Bitcoin for years. In May 2023, Tether said it would funnel up to 15% of its net realized operating profits into Bitcoin, and that it would custody the coins itself rather than rely on third-party custodians.

The strategy has been fueled by staggering profits: the company reported $1.5 billion in net operating profit in the third quarter alone, and it has posted billion-dollar quarterly profits throughout the year while its USDT supply has kept growing.

The broader reserve picture, as laid out in the Q3 report, shows $191.45 billion in assets against $183.50 billion in liabilities, leaving an excess cushion of $7.95 billion, nearly double the $4.11 billion buffer it reported in June. U.S. Treasury bills remain the bulk of reserves at about $132.4 billion.

The transparency gap is a sensitive subject for Tether, which has faced years of scrutiny over whether USDT is fully backed. The company began its first full financial audit with KPMG this year, a long-awaited step toward addressing those doubts.

Ardoino also used the Lugano stage to tout Tether’s scale, saying the company now serves more than 730 million users worldwide and is adding roughly 30 million wallets a quarter, as he pitched a suite of open-source technologies funded by its profits.

#5079. How This New Orleans Hotel Went From Former Haunted House to Chic Destination

The Greek Revival mansion that sits at the corner of Prytania Street and Jackson Avenue in New Orleans’ Garden District wouldn’t know “dull” if it came up and rang the doorbell. The 1857 building’s first owner was a cotton merchant who died of yellow fever. Another owner was fatally struck by lightning. Fast-forward to the 21st century, when a Tina Turner impersonator bought the property and turned it into Magnolia Mansion, a B&B with suites with names like Vampire Lovers’ Lair and Moulin Rouge. A former professional boxer and local politics kingmaker took over, but things fell apart around the time of the FBI’s raid on the property.

Oh, and the estate is said to be haunted.

The Whit's moody mahogany-paneled library

Named in its B&B era by USA Today as one of the top places in the nation “to sleep with a ghost” the magisterial Harris-Maginnis House earned a spot on the city’s ghost-tour circuit with an ever-mounting pile of lore. According to a spooky tour-operator website, guests at the B&B reported various eerie happenings over the years, such as the sensation of somebody tucking them in at night or a presence watching them while they slept. There were also rumblings of mysterious footsteps and slamming doors. Ghosts or no ghosts, the new team taking over the place found gothic-coded wall murals and dark purple velvet hanging at every turn.

Who you gonna call to give an old haunt a new design vernacular? Gretchen Trauth, CEO of the New Orleans investment firm behind the New Orleans hotel, rang up Jensen Logan and Katie Leblanc Killen of Logan Killen, a local design firm known for infusing spaces with a beguiling mix of polish and patina. “The goal was a hotel where nobody can quite tell whether a designer has touched it,” says Logan.

Breakfast room with Pierre Frey wallpaper

“We wanted it to feel very storied, like it’s been someone’s house for a long time,” adds Killen. “There are these layered histories imbued in the furnishings and the art and accessories.”

Named for poet Walt Whitman, who spent three months in New Orleans around the time of the home’s construction, the designers have reimagined the property with a romantic, lived-in, and curio-studded aesthetic that tempts guests to sit down and stay awhile. Architect Seamus McGuire of New Orleans firm Cicada helped navigate historical codes and redraw the map to capture some of the glamour that had drained away in recent years. His blueprint called for converting a trio of bedrooms into lounges. A mahogany-paneled library is now a gallery of antique dog portraits and a repository of volumes from local independent Faulkner House Books. “It’s dark and moody, and where you might want to have a cocktail or a whiskey,” Logan says of the clubby room. Bookworms might also gravitate to the sunroom’s banquette seating, one of the wicker chairs on the wraparound porch, or the breakfast room, a delightfully matchy-matchy enclave covered in Pierre Frey Les Colonnes wallpaper and curtains.

The hardest room to get right, say the designers, was the main lounge. “It’s a giant space that isn’t always in use, and can be tricky to give life to when it’s empty,” says Killen. Intended to serve as a great room by day and an atmospheric cocktail bar come evening, the team layered in elements such as blue paint that reads ethereal by day and warm by night, a custom grid carpet to add a sense of tailoring to the expansive area, and an intimate salon-style wall of works by Louisiana artists, including Robert Moreland and Mallory Page.

Another tightrope walk was the second-floor bar. Landmark rules forbid faking history a space never had, so the team went openly Deco instead. “What we don’t want is to put a contemporary bar into a very historic property,” says McGuire. “You can’t give a false sense of history to a space.”

It will probably come as little surprise that the bedrooms resist uniformity. In addition to the classically designed units, a quartet of cozy garden rooms has low ceilings and glossy oxblood walls; three cabana rooms feel airy, with bamboo beds and high ceilings. “It’s not meant to feel like everything was changed by the same person at the same time,” Killen says of the narrative that she and Logan have introduced to the New Orleans hotel.

As for the supposed ghosts, nobody’s heard boo.

#5078. US midterms: Prediction markets favor Democrats in key crypto races

Kalshi and Polymarket price a 75% chance of a Democratic sweep, days after markets misjudged Brazil’s runoff.

Update (Nov. 3 at 10:32 p.m. UTC): This article has been updated to include comments from Talos.

As Americans across the country head to the polls for the US midterm elections Tuesday, prediction market traders have spent months wagering on the outcome. According to an analysis by The Washington Post, nearly $1.9 billion in election-related contracts had traded on Kalshi and Polymarket as of Monday, marking record trading activity for a midterm election cycle.

Markets on both platforms favor Democrats winning control of Congress, with a Democratic sweep priced at 75% on Kalshi and 75.5% on Polymarket as of 4:30 pm UTC. Kalshi traders gave Democrats a 93% chance of taking the House and a 75% chance of winning the Senate, while Polymarket priced those outcomes at 94.5% and 75.5%, respectively.

The vote comes nine days after prediction markets failed to anticipate the outcome of Brazil’s presidential election, with Flávio Bolsonaro favored at roughly 80% the day before incumbent President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva won the runoff. Kalshi CEO Tarek Mansour reportedly cautioned that “a 5% probability doesn’t mean it won’t happen,” according to CNBC.

For the crypto industry, a shift in congressional control could reshape the prospects for long-awaited regulatory clarity.

A key race for crypto has centered on the Senate seat in Ohio, where Defend American Jobs, part of the Fairshake super PAC network, allocated 54% of its total expenses. According to the official website, $36.9 million was spent on supporting Senator Jon Husted and $19.7 million opposing the staunch crypto critic, Democrat Sherrod Brown. Fairshake spent $40 million to help unseat Brown in 2024.

However, the flood of spending may be to no avail, with traders expecting a win for Brown. The Kalshi market for the race predicted a 72% chance for a Brown win and 72.5% on Polymarket, both in line with a final New York Times/Siena poll that had him ahead 49% to 44%.

A Brown victory could complicate the crypto industry’s legislative agenda, particularly if Democrats regain control of the Senate. Analysts have identified him as a potential chairman of the Senate Agriculture Committee, which oversees the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, although the leadership position remains uncertain.

Related: From Uptober to Hacktober: Are Bitcoin’s seasonal patterns becoming obsolete?

In Maine, however, a Democratic win predicted by traders could help the crypto industry expand its influence in Washington. Republican Senator Susan Collins was one of four Republicans who voted against advancing the CLARITY Act in September, stalling the bill in the Senate.

Kalshi gives Democrat Troy Jackson a 71% chance of unseating her, while Polymarket puts his chances at 71.5%, broadly in line with New York Times polls conducted in October that had Jackson ahead by two percentage points. Advocacy group Stand with Crypto rates Jackson as strongly supportive of crypto, potentially giving the industry another ally in the Senate should he win.

Although many prediction markets are broadly in line with polling results, traders have made clearer calls in three states where polls show close races.

In Alaska, Kalshi gives Democrat Mary Peltola a 71% chance of defeating Republican Senator Dan Sullivan, even though the final New York Times/Siena poll had her ahead by just two percentage points, 48% to 46%. Both candidates have expressed strong support for crypto, according to Stand With Crypto.

In South Carolina, where the candidates were tied at 48% in the New York Times/Siena poll, Kalshi gives Republican Senator Darline Graham, a crypto supporter, a 71% chance of defeating Democrat Annie Andrews.

Iowa remains a toss-up on both platforms, with Democrat Josh Turek overtaking Republican Representative Ashley Hinson on Polymarket at 52%, after months of Hinson being favored to win.

In other key races, prediction markets are broadly in line with the polls. In Michigan, Kalshi gives Democrat Abdul El-Sayed a 79% chance of defeating Republican Mike Rogers. Kalshi also gives Democrat James Talarico a 71% chance of defeating Republican Ken Paxton in Texas, while the final New York Times/Siena poll had Talarico ahead 50% to 43%. A Paxton loss would be a setback for the Digital Freedom Fund, a super PAC backed by Gemini’s co-founders Cameron and Tyler Winklevoss that spent more than $81,000 supporting his campaign. However, Talarico has also expressed support for the crypto industry.

Related: What’s at stake for crypto as the US votes in the 2026 midterms?

Despite the markets leaning towards a Democrat win in both the House and Senate, researchers from Talos have pointed to thin liquidity behind the contracts. According to Talos’ head of research, Kelly Ye, as of 11:30 am ET, just $300,000 of selling would have pushed Kalshi’s Democrats-win-Senate contract down ten points.

The median trade on the markets came in at around $10, with a few large-ticket trades making up the majority of the liquidity behind the contracts.

“As midterm results come in tonight, prediction markets will reprice quickly, but those prices are only as good as the liquidity behind them,” she said in a statement sent to Cointelegraph.

“Whatever happens tonight, one result won’t tell you whether these markets are accurate. Across many settled contracts, outcomes have broadly tracked prices.”

Markets are also starting to look beyond today’s vote to the political and economic landscape following a potential Democratic sweep of Congress. Polymarket’s conditional markets suggest traders are pricing in a higher risk of recession under Democratic control of both chambers, with the implied probability of a US recession by the end of 2027 at 35% if Democrats sweep and 19% if they do not, as of 4:50 pm UTC.

#5076. CFTC clears stock perpetual futures for trading on US exchanges

US regulators are opening the door to stock-based perpetual futures, extending a popular crypto trading product into traditional markets amid ongoing legal disputes.

Perpetual futures contracts tied to individual stocks can begin trading on regulated US exchanges for the first time, according to the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC), extending a derivatives product popularized in cryptocurrency markets into traditional equities.

CFTC Chairman Michael Selig said in a Tuesday statement that the contracts were deemed approved under CFTC Regulation 40.3 and could be offered on exchanges registered with both the CFTC and Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) to trade security futures products.

Selig said bringing the products into regulated US markets would allow American traders to access perpetual futures under US regulatory safeguards rather than being “forced to turn to offshore, and often unregulated, venues.”

Perpetual futures allow traders to take positions on an asset’s price without owning the underlying asset or facing an expiration date. Crypto exchange BitMEX helped popularize the instruments, commonly referred to as perps, in 2016.

CFTC announces approval of equity perpetual futures trading. Source: CFTC

The announcement follows a wave of regulatory filings in September, when Coinbase, Kalshi and Kraken parent Payward, through its Bitnomial exchange, sought approval to offer perpetual futures tied to individual US stocks. OG.com joined the push days later, proposing contracts that could trade around the clock on weekdays.

The filings followed the CFTC’s May approval of Bitcoin perpetual futures contracts for Kalshi and separate regulatory relief allowing Coinbase to provide access to certain cryptocurrency perpetual futures listed on an affiliated foreign exchange.

Selig did not identify which exchanges would offer the contracts or provide a timeline for when trading would begin.

Related: CFTC sues Utah to block action against prediction markets

The announcement also comes nearly three months after President Donald Trump said the CFTC was working to bring Hyperliquid, a decentralized crypto exchange best known for its perpetual futures trading, into the US in a “fully compliant and legal fashion.”

Trump made the remarks during an Aug. 19 White House event. At the time, neither the CFTC nor Hyperliquid had announced a formal plan for bringing the platform to US markets.

However, the CFTC’s approach to perpetual futures has faced legal opposition. In June, CME Group’s Chicago Mercantile Exchange sued the regulator over its approval of cryptocurrency perpetual futures, arguing that the contracts should be regulated as swaps rather than futures and that the agency had exceeded its authority. The distinction matters because swaps are subject to different regulatory requirements, potentially including stricter reporting, margin and registration rules, while futures can trade under the existing futures exchange framework.

The CFTC moved to dismiss the lawsuit in September, arguing that CME lacked standing because it had not demonstrated financial harm and could list similar contracts itself.

Meanwhile, the Capital Markets Alliance, a coalition that includes Cboe, Citadel Securities and Healthy Markets, filed a lawsuit Tuesday afternoon seeking to overturn the CFTC’s approval.

The group argues that the contracts are security-based swaps under the SEC’s jurisdiction and that the CFTC violated the Administrative Procedure Act by failing to adequately explain its decision or consider the potential impact on investors. The lawsuit also seeks to prevent the approvals from taking effect while the case proceeds.

#5075. OpenAI Wants to Rebrand AI Data Centers, Use Them to 'Protect the Grid'

OpenAI Wants to Rebrand AI Data Centers, Use Them to 'Protect the Grid'

OpenAI wants its massive AI data centers to help keep the lights on when electricity grids come under pressure. And, apparently, it would also prefer that people stop calling them AI data centers.

The ChatGPT developer announced its Grid Protectors Program on Monday, a national initiative designed to let future AI facilities reduce electricity consumption during emergencies and periods of high demand, potentially lowering costs for other customers.

Under the plan, OpenAI would coordinate with utility companies to determine how much electricity its facilities could temporarily stop using, how quickly they could respond, and for how long. The company is also exploring on-site batteries and power generation to help manage demand.

“Families and small businesses should not be asked to subsidize a compute campus’s electricity needs,” OpenAI said in its announcement.

The program addresses one of the biggest complaints surrounding the AI infrastructure boom: companies are building increasingly power-hungry facilities while residents worry about paying higher—and water—electricity bills to support them.

OpenAI is using a planned facility in Effingham County, Georgia, as its first example. The company has signed a 25-year agreement with Georgia Power covering approximately 3,200 megawatts of electricity demand, with up to 1,000 megawatts available for temporary reductions during periods of high demand.

Under the approved agreement, OpenAI must cover the full infrastructure costs required to serve its facility, rather than passing those expenses on to existing customers.

Georgia Power also projects approximately $950 million in annual customer savings beginning in 2029, equivalent to at least $15 per month for a typical residential customer. However, those estimates cover the utility's broader portfolio of large electricity customers and expected growth, not just OpenAI's project.

The Georgia facility is still under development, and the savings remain projections. OpenAI has not yet disclosed binding demand-reduction commitments for other facilities under its national program.

OpenAI's electricity initiative also arrives after reports of a massive PR campaign to change what people call the facilities in the first place to erase from collective memory the negative sentiment towards AI data centers.

According to Axios, OpenAI is preparing a post-midterm policy and PR campaign that includes rebranding data centers as "AI compute campuses," an effort to make the infrastructure sound more appealing to communities, effectively trying to solve a problem by changing the problem’s name.

The company has already started using the terminology. Its Grid Protectors announcement repeatedly refers to future facilities as "compute campuses," although it occasionally uses the more familiar term "data centers."

An OpenAI spokesperson confirmed the naming strategy to Business Insider, arguing that the term better reflects the facilities' broader role in computing, energy infrastructure, and skilled employment.

The reported rebranding comes as opposition to AI infrastructure projects has become a political issue across the United States. Pennsylvania introduced new restrictions on large data centers in August, including provisions designed to protect residents from higher electricity costs. Other states are following.

OpenAI and six other major technology companies also signed a White House pledge in March to pay for the electricity and grid upgrades their AI projects require.

The new program goes further by proposing that AI facilities could become useful to utilities rather than simply adding pressure to their systems. But the national framework is still largely an ambition, with individual agreements to be negotiated.

OpenAI's Georgia project will provide an early test of that approach. Electricity delivery to the planned 3.2-gigawatt facility is scheduled to begin in 2028 and expand in phases through 2032.

#5074. SpaceX blocks tokenized stock listing on OKXICE despite offshore trading boom

SpaceX joined several other issuers objecting to OKXICE tokenized trading, months after crypto platforms offered tokens tied to its shares.

Just months after tokenized versions of SpaceX shares became one of the biggest drivers of onchain stock trading, the aerospace company is pushing back against plans to bring similar trading to US investors.

According to a Nov. 2 public notice from OKXICE, SpaceX has objected to the proposed listing of tokenized versions of its shares on the trading venue, a joint venture between crypto exchange OKX and New York Stock Exchange parent Intercontinental Exchange (ICE). The company now joins Tesla and Amazon among 56 companies objecting to the proposed listings.

Under the Securities and Exchange Commission’s five-year Innovation Exemption introduced in September, platforms such as OKXICE can offer trading in tokenized versions of US stocks without registering as national securities exchanges. But the framework also gives companies a say in the process: They have 30 days to object to the proposed trading of their shares tokenized by third parties, effectively keeping those tokens off the venue.

Tokenized stocks are blockchain-based representations of company shares that allow investors to gain exposure to traditional equities onchain, often around the clock. Depending on the structure, they can be backed by actual shares, although the rights granted to tokenholders may differ from those of traditional shareholders.

OKXICE co-chair and former New York Governor Andrew Cuomo told Cointelegraph on Oct. 20 that the venture expected more objections as companies avoided being the first to test the model.

SpaceX’s objection comes despite tokenized versions of its shares already trading on platforms outside the US, where they have attracted significant trading activity since the company’s June public offering.

Ahead of the SpaceX listing, Backpack Securities, a stock brokerage and tokenization platform, and crypto exchange Kraken, through its xStocks offering, both prepared tokenized versions of the aerospace company’s stock, each designed to be backed by actual shares. Backpack’s SPCX token went on to record $1.08 billion in onchain trading volume in June, while Kraken’s SPCXx token recorded $852 million.

Unlike Backpack and Kraken, OKXICE would open tokenized stock trading to US investors under the SEC’s new regulatory framework, potentially tapping into the world’s largest equity market by capitalization and expanding the investor base for tokenized securities.

Nasdaq warned issuers in September that they “must be cautious about their participation” in the venture, arguing that tokenized stock trading could fragment liquidity and undermine long-term shareholder value.

Despite the growing opposition, Cuomo said during an Oct. 30 press conference that as people see the benefits of tokenization, “you’ll see more and more companies participate.”

#5073. This Shell-Shaped Villa Has Mediterranean Views and a Windswept Feel

When designer Federica Gosio talks about this project in Sardinia, she describes it as “an arch over the sea.” Gosio—who leads the firm bearing her name and is also a cofounder of Studio MILO—explains, “the truly unique aspect of this home is its ability to adapt to its surroundings, the sea, and nature.” Overlooking the bay of Porto Rafael, an area on the island’s northern coast famous for its remote location and its scenic beauty, the house resembles a seashell.

The villa looks like a white shell that has emerged from the sea to embrace the surrounding landscape .

There is no single guiding principle in Gosio’s design; instead, the shell-shaped villa embodies a capacity to adapt: “The aim is to give shape to ever-changing realities, integrating it with strength and grace into a larger context,” she says. It’s an approach that characterizes many of Gosio’s projects. This one springs from the rugged land where the home is located and pays homage to its setting through its design.

A custom-made bench follows the perimeter of the portico, wrapping around the space. At its center is a set of furnishings in wood and neutral-toned fabrics.

Working with Gosio, Alessandra Radaelli, the owner of this shell-shaped villa, created an organic, sculptural retreat. “We decided to honor the design legacy of Rafael Neville,” she says, referring to the founder of Porto Rafael. That meant “favoring a harmonious approach in which architecture blends with the landscape.”

Interior designer Federica Gosio on the terrace of this unique and intriguing villa.

The villa is characterized by curved lines and pure white surfaces that also have a textured quality. Rattan and straw accents add warmth to the space.

The home’s pointed arches, organic geometries, and natural materials were inspired by the earth and the sea. “The minimalist spaces were designed to embrace the sunlight, the wind, and the slow rhythm of island life,” Gosio explains. “It’s a project that interprets the Mediterranean in a contemporary way through a refined balance of memory, nature, and design.”

Every corner of this villa blends seamlessly with its surroundings: In this outdoor space, the shapes soften and the lines curve. At the center is a white rattan lounge set.

With its soft curves, the home feels as if it has been shaped by the winds that blow across Sardinia. During the winter, the Mistral here is powerful, sweeping across the island and reshaping the landscape. It looks almost as if the same process has taken place both inside and outside the villa. The reality is that this effect was a deliberate design choice to soften the edges of the villa and smooth out its different volumes.

This semicircular arch draws one’s gaze to the sea. In the foreground are two coffee tables by Mogg. In the background, on a terrace, rattan armchairs complement the organic curves of the seaside architecture.

Both inside and out, the villa is a whitewashed structure that follows the contours of the land. Gosio and her team have done away with straight lines, opting instead for a softer silhouette. The signature element of the home is undoubtedly the pointed arch, used for both the villa’s doors and its hallways. “The soft volumes, textured surfaces, and carefully placed rocks guide the visitor toward the entrance with its hand-brushed, iron arched door, custom-designed by Daniele Mingardo,” Gosio says.

The property’s garden is an idyllic green expanse leading down to the sea with junipers and various plants native to the Mediterranean framing the villa.

Interior designer Federica Gosio (on the left) and homeowner Alessandra Radaelli.

Surrounding the home are acres of land covered in lush Mediterranean vegetation leading down to the sea, with the route including green terraces and social spaces, including the panoramic pool and an outdoor kitchen. The feeling is that of an earthly Eden planted in a corner of Sardinia.

The organic arch reveals a living space featuring an Asawa sofa by Secolo, Dune side tables by Mogg, and a vintage rattan pendant lamp purchased on the Pamono website. The Paysage II rug is by Atelier Février, the blue ceramic Anfora Anulare vase is by Walter Usai, and the cups and saucers are by LR Ceramics.

The goal of respecting and celebrating the villa’s setting is also reflected in the colors and materials used throughout the home. “The white walls, the floors in Orosei marble with a bush hammered finish and ‘broken edges,’ and the organically shaped furnishings come together to create a measured and well-proportioned design, inspired by the neutral and warm tones of the soil here,” Gosio says.

Access to the kitchen is also through a large, sweeping arch. The custom oak cabinets are paired with WA ceramic tiles by Decoratori Bassanesi on the wall. The table, which resembles a sculpture, is the Luna Elíptica by La Nena.

Orosei stone, which is found on the coast of Sardinia, not only forms the floors but also the sinks (both indoor and outdoor), a clear example of how the island was integrated into the design. Rounding out the composition is a selection of vintage rattan pieces and sculptural creations by Pretziada, a creative design practice based in Sardinia, and its artisans. Nothing is left to chance and every element contributes to the home’s exquisite design and its appealing composition.

At the center of the dining area, which overlooks the outdoor space, is a Luna Elíptica table by La Nena. Next to it is a Lia Chair by Chiara Andreatti and Pierpaolo Mandis for Pretziada, a piece that is both handcrafted and contemporary, with roots in traditional Sardinian design. The Holden Linear Pendant Uplight is from Porta Romana. On the table are several ceramic pieces by LR Ceramics.

The infinity-edge pool blends seamlessly into the villa’s architecture. Alongside it is a sundeck surrounded by lush green foliage and aromatic plants.

The outdoor spaces in this villa are extensions of its interior. Wooden furnishings with a distinct nautical flair are set amid breathtaking green spaces with ocean views. On the table is a Mini Geen-A lamp by Kartell.

A custom outdoor kitchen with views of the sea. The sink is made of Orosei marble.

The pool has views of the sea and sunsets.

The primary bedroom, with its organic lines, overlooks the pool and, beyond it, the sea. The custom headboard is upholstered in a striped fabric. Next to it is a Holden Wall Light from Porta Romana.

In the guest room, the custom headboard sets the tone. Next to it is a pendant lamp with a rattan Braided Lampshade-Bottle by Ferm Living. Atop a stone nightstand, a vase by LR Ceramics.

In this bath, Totem tiles from the Nok collection by 41zero42 add texture to the wall. The sink is by Catalano, and the faucets are from the Sirius series by Rubinetterie Stella.

This bath, with its rich textural quality, is defined by the wall-mounted sink in Orosei stone paired with a Roma faucet set by Rubinetterie Stella.

This room evokes traditional Sardinian design with a custom headboard that’s a mosaic of shapes and neutral tones, upholstered in Zinc’s Cubilete Natural fabric. The Holden Wall Light is from Porta Romana, as is the Huxley Jordan Wall Light reading lamp.

#5072. Women’s Heart Health Just Got a Major Update. Here’s What Doctors Want You to Know

This is a much-needed and overdue change.

Big news in female heart health: Four major health organizations have come together to create a shared framework and clinical guidance for women's heart health across several different life stages.

The American Heart Association, American Academy of Pediatrics, American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists (ACOG), and Society for Maternal-Fetal Medicine came together to outline the “female life stage" framework to protect heart health from puberty through menopause and beyond. The framework (which essentially acts as a classification system for medical providers) was just published in the journal Circulation.

This is a big deal for many reasons, including acknowledging that female heart health and risk factors change over a woman's life. "This has been a long time coming," says Nicole Weinberg, MD, a cardiologist at Pacific Heart Institute’s Women’s Heart Center in Santa Monica, California.

According to the American Heart Association, cardiovascular disease is the leading cause of death in women, claiming more lives than breast cancer, lung cancer, and chronic lung disease combined. But only four in 10 women are aware that cardiovascular disease is the biggest threat to their health.

This new framework separates cardiovascular risk factors, along with care needs, throughout the different stages of a woman's life. Here's what you need to know.

Meet the experts: Sadiya Khan, MD, chair of the collaborative writing group and professor of Cardiovascular Epidemiology at Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine; Jennifer Haythe, MD, co-director of the Women’s Heart Center at NewYork-Presbyterian/Columbia University Irving Medical Center; and Nicole Weinberg, MD, cardiologist at Pacific Heart Institute’s Women’s Heart Center in Santa Monica, California

The Female Life Stage framework is defined by hormone changes specific to girls and women at different stages of life, hence the name. The framework also recognizes pregnancy as an event that can impact cardiovascular health at any or several life stages. Here's a breakdown of each:

Adopting this new way of classifying female life stages “offers a transformative opportunity to equitably improve cardiovascular health for all girls and women," the researchers wrote in the review article.

This has never been done before. “For the first time, four major medical groups agree that a woman’s heart health is shaped by every stage of her life, from her first period to menopause," says Jennifer Haythe, MD, co-director of the Women’s Heart Center at NewYork-Presbyterian/Columbia University Irving Medical Center.

This helps address the "fragmentation of care" that can happen between ob-gyns, cardiologists, and other specialists who care for women, says Sadiya Khan, MD, chair of the collaborative writing group and professor of Cardiovascular Epidemiology at Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine. "We're all speaking different languages and we're all speaking different levels of care, yet they're all connected," she says.

The framework is a "shared playbook" that doctors from different specialties can use to guide patient care, along with a language that's understandable and easy to communicate for everyone, Dr. Khan says. "It's really connecting those aspects of health that we think are disconnected," she says.

This new system helps to bridge the gap between viewing cardiovascular care as something completely separate from women's health. "This is meant to reframe cardiovascular health as part of a woman's care," says Dr. Khan. "Any of these things may influence the other."

Weinberg says the framework will create "a more unified view" of women's health. "By creating a road map that is shared by pediatricians, cardiologists, ob-gyns, and maternal-fetal medicine doctors, things like PMOS, irregular periods, pregnancy complications, and metabolic shifts in perimenopause will be treated as real heart health signals, so early warning signs aren’t missed," Haythe says.

While covering women’s health there, she discovered her passion for health service journalism and took a break to get her Masters in Public Health. Post-grad school, she worked as a freelance writer and as The Daily Beast’s first Beauty, Health, and Wellness Reporter.

Carina is an alum of the Fashion Institute of Technology and the Yale School of Public Health. She and her French Bulldog, Bao Bao, split their time between Brooklyn and Connecticut. She enjoys reformer Pilates, (slow) running, and smelling the fancy toiletries in boutique fitness class locker rooms.

#5071. RUNE rallies 15% as THORChain restarts most swaps after halt

THORChain has restored trading on Bitcoin, Ethereum and several other networks following a refund bug, while three chains remain paused.

THORChain’s RUNE token rose about 15% over the past 24 hours as the cross-chain swap protocol restored trading on most networks following a weekend halt triggered by an Ethereum block reorganization.

THORChain (RUNE) was trading at around $0.64 on Wednesday, up approximately 15% over the previous day, according to CoinMarketCap.

THORChain’s network status page showed trading and transaction signing marked “OK” on 10 of its 13 listed external networks. BNB Smart Chain, Cosmos Hub and Solana continued to show warnings for both functions.

Bitcoin, Ethereum, Bitcoin Cash, Dogecoin, Litecoin, Tron and Zcash were among the networks showing trading was re-enabled. Avalanche, Base and XRP were already operating when Cointelegraph reported on the halt on Monday.

The disruption began on Saturday when a one-block Ethereum reorganization removed a 10 Ether deposit that THORChain had already credited, according to the protocol’s account.

After the swap failed to meet its price limit, THORChain issued a refund against funds its vault had never received. The sender’s actual deposit was also refunded, resulting in an extra 10 ETH payout and triggering an automatic pause.

Node operators subsequently paused trading across multiple networks while developers investigated.

The halt also disrupted attempts to move funds stolen in the $6.3 billion Binance hack, according to blockchain analytics company Chainalysis, which said the pause had inadvertently delayed laundering by North Korean hackers.

The partial restart restores access to several cross-chain swap routes.

#5069. Reigning Champ Hellen Obiri Didn’t Change Much About This Year’s Prep for the NYC Marathon

Her secret weapon to a successful training block? A lot of naps and even more tea.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Speaking from personal experience, the closer you get to marathon day, the louder the worries become. Can I really make it to the start line when I’m so sore and tired all the time? Are my long runs and weekly mileage on par with everyone else’s? Do I even want to be doing this anymore? That is, unless you’re the reigning champion, Hellen Obiri.

This weekend, she’ll be making her return to the New York City Marathon—which she won last year in a course record of 2 hours, 19 minutes, 51 seconds. When I chatted with the On athlete a few weeks out from the race, she had just wrapped up a morning session of 12.4 miles and still had another five on deck later that day. It seems like a lot, but “the evening session is always very easy,” she assured me.

Obiri, 36, has won four marathons (NYC and Boston, two times each), and though she knows everyone will be watching to see if she’ll do it again, she doesn’t seem affected by the pressure. “When you're the reigning champion, everybody's looking at you and wondering what you can do,” she says. “You have that pressure of, ‘How can I win back to back?’”

But Obiri knows that in a marathon, anything can happen. It’s a long distance, and she’s competing against the best of the best—so she’s not counting a single person out. “For me, [the key] is to keep training well, and to keep that in my mind: I'm the defending champion,” Obiri says. “The most important thing is to stay safe and to stay healthy, and then everything's possible.”

Here’s what she’s doing to prepare for race day—and her advice to those heading to a starting line soon.

Because, well, it worked so well in 2025. “Sometimes we do study who is in the race,” she admits, though she didn’t name drop any one contender she has her eye on this year.

Each week, she logs over 100 miles, and gives herself two to three days to recover after a long run or speed session. (Recovery days still often consist of easy miles, though.) Her longest run heading into November 1? Twenty-four miles. Even the best of the best don’t run the full distance until race day.

She also preps—both physically and mentally—for pushing through particularly hard moments. Will she still have enough in the tank to keep pushing the pace with her speedy competitors come mile 20? What about in those last 100 meters if she needs to dig deep for a sprint finish?

It’s her second year working with On Athletic Club coach Laura Thweatt, who also boasts an impressive history with the NYC Marathon, placing eighth in 2021. Having a coach who has also raced New York has made a huge difference, Obiri says, because she knows what it’s like to train for this specific race and also what it takes to do well. Plus, Thweatt is able to join her on runs (though she won’t be running the race herself). “She’s my training partner,” Obiri says.

Thweatt is also Obiri’s first-ever female coach, and that’s not to be underestimated, either. “To have a female coach, you can't hide something from her,” Obiri says. “She already knows everything, what you're going through in your mind, in your body.”

Beyond running, Obiri does strength work three times per week, focusing on lower-body muscles like her calves and glutes, but surprisingly, she’s pretty focused on her arms, too. At her level, everything counts, including how strong your arms are when you’re swinging them during a race—an area that needs some improvement, she says.

She naps. Often. After her morning session, Obiri takes a snooze. Then, she heads to lunch. After lunch? She’s napping again. “I sleep a lot,” she says. Obiri estimates that she spends anywhere from five to six hours a day napping.

For Obiri, the basics—sleeping, eating, and hydrating—are the most important things for feeling her best. And when it comes to hydration, Obiri’s not getting caught up in the electrolyte craze or downing gallons of water. Instead, she relies on tea as her main source for fluids. She drinks all different kinds, but will make it in a big batch, complete with milk and three spoonfuls of sugar per cup.

“I’m addicted to tea with sugar,” she says. She has about seven cups per day, including before a run. It’s an easy way to get some extra carbs in, for sure. Along with her tea, before her 6:45 a.m. run, Obiri will have two slices of bread and a banana. “I can’t go on my runs with an empty stomach,” she says.

“The most important thing is to listen to your body,” she says. You might be feeling the excitement right off the bat or trying to keep up with the crowd, but remember to pay attention to how you feel and not go too hard too soon. “Don't go crazy because it's a marathon—it's a long journey—and needs patience,” she says. Make sure you’re keeping on top of fluids and fuel, too.

At this point, you may be deep into training and feeling drained—that’s completely normal, but does require a mindset shift. When Obiri is lacking motivation, she just tells herself she has to do it, or at least start it. “If I start, I will finish it,” she says. And, when a run gets hard? No matter how far she is from the end, she tells herself, “I’m almost there.”

It’s possible your For You Page (like mine) is filled with people freaking out about where they’re at in training—and it can be pretty easy to compare yourself to them. Maybe you have friends crushing long runs on Strava and are starting to wonder if it says anything about your progress.

Whatever it is, stay focused on your own program. “Everybody trains differently, so just know you have trained well and focus on your body, and trust me, you’ll be at the finish line and run well,” Obiri says.

Ultimately, your marathon is about you and what you did to get there. “Trust yourself, trust what you have been training for, and be ready to do what you have been training for six months or longer,” Obiri says.

#5068. 10 Best Airbnbs in London With Brilliant Design (2026)

If London excels at anything, it’s lodgings with soul—from the silver-service glamour of Claridge’s to the shiny new scenes of At Sloane. But these days, it’s the city’s next generation of stays—creative, design-led rentals that might include a sweet stay tucked into a mews, a contemporary warehouse with exposed brick, or a period town house on a leafy street—that capture the true spirit of living like a local. In a place where nightly rates can soar well into four figures, claiming your own corner of the city feels more like the move. Here are the London Airbnbs where you can carry on like a local—homes that are made for sinking back into after a morning finding the best coffee or after a late night in Soho.

Guided by a minimal color palette, light-toned wood weaves cohesion throughout the interiors, which are outfitted with architectural details and decor accents. On cloudless days, sunlight bathes the upper levels, giving the wood flourishes a warm glow. Just steps away from the refrigerator, a hidden walk-in shower, sauna, and spare bathroom are discreetly tucked away. The owners describe this home as a “domestic gallery,” but its beauty doesn’t make it any less liveable. “Every detail is thoughtfully considered,” says shopping editor Audrey Lee. “A Space Residence reimagines mundane fixtures into modern design moments that make you feel like you’re living in art. #5066. Judge Orders Bitcoin Wallet Returned to Family of Convicted Canadian Killer

Judge Orders Bitcoin Wallet Returned to Family of Convicted Canadian Killer

A Canadian judge has ordered that a laptop containing a Bitcoin wallet be returned to the family of William Sandeson, who is serving a life sentence for the 2015 murder of fellow university student Taylor Samson during a drugs deal, Global News reported.

Justice John Bodurtha of the Nova Scotia Supreme Court ruled on Tuesday that prosecutors had not proved the cryptocurrency was linked to the killing. Sandeson, who appeared virtually at the hearing in Halifax, had filed a motion in April seeking the return of personal items seized when police searched his apartment after his arrest in 2015.

"The Crown has not met its burden to demonstrate the cryptocurrency was used as part of the drug transaction, proceeds of crime, or otherwise tainted by criminality," Bodurtha said, adding that its potential use in the deal was "hypothetical."

The judge did rule that about $3,600 (C$5,000) in cash found in Sandeson's apartment after the murder was "offence-related property," meaning it will not be returned.

Sandeson has said the Bitcoin was worth between about $5,800 and $7,200 (C$8,000 to C$10,000), according to Global News. A lawyer representing Samson's family in a separate wrongful death lawsuit, in which the cryptocurrency had been at the center, has said it could now be worth more than $144,000 (C$200,000). Global News did not report how much Bitcoin the wallet holds.

Sandeson, then 23 and about to start medical school at Dalhousie University, arranged for Samson, 22, to come to his apartment on August 15, 2015, to sell him cannabis that Sandeson had arranged to buy for C$40,000, according to prosecutors at his 2023 trial. Sandeson shot Samson, kept the drugs and the money, and later admitted to dumping his body in a river that feeds the Bay of Fundy. Samson's remains have never been found.

Sandeson was convicted of first-degree murder in 2017, but an appeals court overturned the verdict in 2020. At a second trial in 2023, he admitted to shooting Samson but said he acted in self-defense. A jury found him guilty of second-degree murder, which carries an automatic life sentence. He is serving his sentence in Quebec and will be eligible for parole in 2030.

#5063. Amber Igiede Needs a Comeback. Good Thing She’s Built For It

The LOVB volleyball pro is sharing her ACL recovery journey in a new video series.

Amber Igiede was at the top of her game. The 25-year-old professional volleyball player had an illustrious college career at the University of Hawaii where she was a two-time All-American. After college, she signed a contract with League One Volleyball, where the middle blocker became one the faces of her team, LOVB Houston.

Now, winning games and breaking records have taken a back seat. Igiede’s focus is recovery.

This spring, Igiede suffered an intense ACL injury that will keep her off the court for the rest of the year. Healing her body—and staging an epic comeback—are her number one priorities.

Women’s Health can exclusively reveal that Igiede and her journey back to volleyball will be featured in Built for the Comeback, a LOVB-original series. The first episode is available now, and it features the athlete through the preliminary, intimate moments of recovery.

“I got really emotional watching it for the first time,” she tells WH. “It’s one of those things I’m savoring.”

Among the multitude of lessons Igiede has learned throughout this process, a principal one has been how to view herself as a person, not just an athlete.

“Anyone that really, truly goes through this knows how misleading the persona of an athlete can be when we’re going through something like this,” she says. “We are taught to ‘fake it until you make it’ and grind through everything.”

It takes Igiede back to when the COVID-19 pandemic hit while she was playing at Hawaii, and the wake-up call that came from a forced break from the sport. Now, she’s relying on the lessons she learned then to get her through this stage of her life and career.

“Every athlete knows that you dedicated so many years to this sport, so it’s a part of you,” she says. “When that part is taken away, there is sort of a grief process, and it’s very much magnified in the beginning.”

With her schedule considerably more open, Igiede says she has been able to explore new hobbies and find new outlets for joy. She says she has rediscovered her love for cooking and has been able to invest more time in her family and loved ones.

One of the best pieces of advice she received throughout her recovery process is to accept what happened to her, feel bad for herself a little, and then move on.

“You can’t spend too much time wallowing in what has happened, because you still need to look forward,” she says.

Her main focus right now is feeling strong. As a middle blocker, leg strength is crucial for Igiede to have the vertical she needs for the position. The main way she stays locked in on her goals to get her strength back is to physically write them down. She says this is how she beats her biggest competitor—herself.

For her specific injury, the recovery process is long. Because it was a complex, multi-ligament tear, the standard recuperation time is around 9-12 months. Igiede’s plan has been extremely methodical, relying on benchmarks to indicate when is the right time to incorporate something new to her regime.

Above all, she still remains grateful for the support from the league and her loved ones.

“It’s always such a privilege to play in the United States,” she says. “I played for the inaugural season [of LOVB], and I’ve always been super grateful that I’m able to play in this league. The opportunities they’ve given me, especially since this injury, they’ve helped so much.”

Looking ahead, Igiede isn’t just hoping to return to the player she was. She wants to come back even better, she says. “Every day is really an opportunity to get better and get back to where you were, or get back to where I can be even better than I was.”

Alyssa Cruz is the social media editor at Women’s Health, where she drives social strategy and helps shape the brand’s voice across platforms. Prior to WH, she worked at CBS News and palabra. Originally from Toledo, she is a proud alumna of Ohio University, where she received two bachelor’s degrees in journalism and Spanish. When she is not creating social content, you can find her trying different restaurants in New York City or planning her next trip.

#5062. 1inch lawyer sees agencies driving US crypto regulation as midterms unfold

Crypto executives expect US regulators to drive crypto policymaking regardless of the midterm election outcome.

With voting underway in the US midterm elections and control of Congress at stake, Maylea Ma, deputy general counsel at 1inch, said she expects federal agencies to drive the next phase of crypto regulation.

The election could reshape the path forward for the stalled CLARITY Act, but Ma said “real movement” in US digital-asset regulation over the next few years will come from the US Securities and Exchange Commission, Commodity Futures Trading Commission and Treasury, rather than Congress.

Ma said those agencies were increasingly engaging with crypto projects. However, agency engagement does not provide the stability the industry had hoped to secure through legislation.

“The long-term goal, of course, remains a statute that cannot be reversed every time a government changes,” said Ma.

That uncertainty also matters to institutional investors, according to Michael Ho, co-founder of D3, the team behind Doma Protocol.

“With the market structure bill stalled, US crypto policy continues to rest on agency guidance that a future administration can reverse, and that keeps serious capital waiting,” Ho said.

“Whatever the result, clear rules written into law are what will bring institutional capital into tokenized real-world assets.”

In August, the SEC proposed a framework for crypto fundraising, including exemptions for certain token offerings and a safe harbor from treatment as investment contracts. In September, it also updated staff guidance covering token buybacks, crypto networks and staking receipt tokens.

The CFTC, meanwhile, submitted a crypto market regulatory plan for White House review on Sept. 17, two days after the Senate failed to advance the CLARITY Act.

#5060. ZachXBT Names French Bank Crédit Mutuel Arkéa as Alleged Victim of $133M Crypto Theft

ZachXBT Names French Bank Crédit Mutuel Arkéa as Alleged Victim of $133M Crypto Theft

Onchain investigator ZachXBT has named French banking group Crédit Mutuel Arkéa as the alleged victim of a crypto theft of more than $133 million.

"Disclosing the EU financial institution exploited for $133M+ is allegedly Credit Mutuel Arkea, a French bank with $235B in assets," ZachXBT wrote on his Telegram channel on Tuesday. "The attack on the bank occurred for multiple days. I suspect the amount stolen is larger when accounting for OTC activity."

Crédit Mutuel Arkéa has not publicly confirmed any theft. Decrypt has reached out to the bank for comment.

The mutual banking group has been preparing to move into crypto. In May, French financial newspaper L'Agefi reported that it had applied for a license under MiCA, the EU's crypto rulebook, to offer crypto services to its retail customers and to provide them as a white-label service through its Arkéa Banking Services division.

In an update earlier on Tuesday, he said he had identified a further $27 million in exchange withdrawals by the attacker, bringing the total to more than $133 million, and that some of those withdrawals took place after his initial disclosure. He also published four Ethereum addresses he said were linked to the theft.

"My working theory is a large financial institution in Western Europe was exploited and it's currently being covered up," ZachXBT wrote at the time, accusing it of not having conducted "proper incident disclosure" and thereby preventing law enforcement from freezing some $30 million in stolen funds.

He did not say how he had identified the bank, or how the attacker gained access to its exchange accounts.

The allegation comes days after the $6.3 billion hack of Binance, the largest crypto theft on record, which the FBI has attributed to North Korean hackers. ZachXBT said the attacker in the suspected EU theft remains unidentified, but that he could say "with a high degree of confidence it is NOT Lazarus Group," the North Korean hacking collective, "based on multiple laundering indicators."
